NEWS & TRENDS 


It’s called the “Home Town Pharmacy Service”—an electronic network of 
independent pharmacies across the country patterned after the FTD flower 
delivery program. The new service would be offered to third-party payors 
who insist on a mail-order option. When prescription orders are received at 
“Home Town” headquarters, they would be transmitted electronically to the 
independent pharmacy nearest to the patient based on ZIP code. The phar- 
macy would then mail or deliver the prescription to the patient. It’s still just 
a twinkle in the eye of NARD executive vice president Charles West, but he 
says this approach “sure makes more sense than having a prescription 


| mailed to you from hundreds of miles away by a faceless, unregulated 
| someone working in a mail-order mill.” 


Pharmaceutical manufacturers who engage in illegal price discrimination 
against retail pharmacists could lose patent protection for their products if 


| the latest legal challenge to multi-tier drug pricing succeeds. The Georgia 

| Pharmacy Association’s Academy of Independent Pharmacy is spearheading 
| that litigation, which has already resulted in suits by 1,346 independents 

| in 15 states. To “cure illegal price discrimination, 
| mandatory licensing of all drug patents at “reasonable royalty rates.” 


" the pharmacists want 


Health and Human Services Inspector General June Gibbs Brown has al- 


| ready warned pharmacists that accepting payments to shift Medicaid pa- 
| tients to drugs favored by 


“incentive program” sponsol1s may constitute 
criminal fraud under the federal anti-kickback laws. Now Justice Depart- 


| ment officials are pushing for an even broader standard. Testifying at re- 

| cent House hearings on pharmaceutical manufacturer promotion programs, 
| Justice Department health care fraud counsel Gerald Stern suggested that 

| the anti-kickback law be expanded beyond Medicaid and Medicare to apply 
| to private health programs as well. 


For the sixth straight year, Americans responding to the Gallup Poll rated 


| pharmacists the nation’s most trusted professionals. This time 62 percent 


gave pharmacists “high” or “very high” marks for “honesty and ethical stan- 
The clergy was a distant runner-up with 52 percent. The least

DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, November 14, 1994 


RE OTC MERCHANDISER SR 
Diabetes: A shot in pharmacys arm 


by Mike Troy 


rofessionally rewarding and 

profitable. Those are two over 

riding reasons for catering to 
the needs of diabetes patients. 

“By working with diabetes pa- 
tients, you can really help change 
their lives. It gives you professional 
satisfaction,” said Bill Devine, owner 
of the Diabetic Shoppe Appleton, 
Wis. The other side of the coin is 
profitability. “We all sell syringes, we 
| all sell insulin, and we all know 
there is no money in that. Slowly 
and surely we expanded our dia 
betes line and as we got into it, we 
realized there was a great need,” 
said Devine. 

His 1,700-square-foot store has a 
24-foot diabetes care section that 
turns eight times a year and gener 
ates $400,000 of the store's $2.5 
million total sales. Impressive re 
sults for even a large drug store. 

Devine is able to achieve that level 
of sales because diabetes patients 
are looking for a retailer who can 
cater to all of their needs, who un 
derstands their disease, who makes 
them feel special and improves their 
quality of life. 

“There is a lot of misinforma- 

tion and lack of knowledge on 
the part of practitioners. They 
are behind the curve as far as 
what is going on,” Devine said. 
“It represents an opportunity for 
pharmacists to be experts in 
their community. You have to 
take the time and effort to go out 
and knock on some doors and to 
work with the other health pro- 
fessionals in your area. 

“After awhile, once they start 
hearing from their patients about 
the wonderful job you are doing, 
you will start to see more referrals.” 


Care today “a disgrace?” 


| Agreeing with Devine is Keith 
| Campbell, a pharmacist, certi- 
fied diabetes educator and asso- 
ciate dean and professor of 
pharmacy practice at Washing- 


ton State University. 

“The care of patients with diabetes 
leaves a lot to be desired and that cre 
ates an opportunity for pharmacists 
because they see the diabetes patient 
seven or eight times more often than 
the doctor,” Campbell said. In fact, 
national studies of diabetes-related 
complications show that “diabetes 
and its treatment in the United States 
is a national disgrace,” he added. 

Diabetes is the third leading cause 
of death in the United States and it 
causes complications that can lead 
to blindness, kidney and 
nerve damage. 

Patients with diabetes tend to ex 
perience feelings of anxiety, frustra 
tion and anger in their quest to lo 
cate health practitioners and 
product suppliers that understand 
their disease. An estimated 96 per 
cent of diabetes patients have never 
seen a specialist and only one out of 
10 has been given instruction on the 
correct use of their blood glucose 
meter. Sixty percent of patients 
make insulin dosing errors, Camp 
bell added. One of 17 diabetes pa 
tients sees an ophthalmologist every 
two years even though diabetes is a 
leading cause of blindness. 


disease 


Sales of strips and meters in drug and food 
stores combined increased 29 percent. 


DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, November 14, 1994 


“If you provide good care to these 
patients, word will get around and it 
will help your business,” Campbell 
said. “There are a lot of things you 
can do to help them by selling dia 
betes care-related products that will 
help the patient feel better.” 

Campbell and Devine recentiy gave 

presentation at the National Asso 
ciation of Retail Druggists (NARD) 
annual meeting held in Boston. 
Devine’s presentation was called, 
“Diabetes: The Niche Market that 
Makes Cents (and Dollars),” and was 
sponsored by McKesson Drug Co. 
Campbell's presentation, titled “Dia 
betes: Managing and Marketing a 
Specialty Service,” was sponsored by 
Becton Dickinson, Boehringer 
Mannheim and Eli Lilly and Co. 


Demand is growing 


The time has never been better to 
get involved in diabetes care say man 
ufacturers and retailers. 
blood glucose strips and meters ar¢ 
booming and showing no signs o! 
slowing. In drug stores and food 
stores combined, sales increased 29 
percent to $355 million from $275.9 
million during the 12-month period 
ended June 1994, according to 
Towne-Oller and Associates. Unit 
volume increased 31 percent dur 
ing the same period, growing to 
9.4 million units from 7.2 million. 

To manufacturers and others 
who closely watch the diabetes 
care market, the sales boom this 
past year wasn't a big surprise. 
There are an estimated 13-14 
million diabetes patients in the 
United States, about 10 percent 
of whom have Type | insulin-de 
pendent diabetes. There are 
believed to be large numbers of 
undiagnosed diabetes cases. 
Growth has come as new pa 
tients are diagnosed and enter 
the market and as existing pa 
tients test more frequently. 

Prompting the move to more 
frequent testing was last year's 
release of a major study by the 
National Institutes of Health

also

Diabetes: A shot in the arm 


Continued from page 23 
Results of the Diabetes Control and 
Complications Trial, also known as 
the DCCT, showed that patients 
who closely monitor and maintain 
tight control of their blood glucose 
levels through frequent testing 
greatly reduce the chances of devel 
oping long-term complications as 
sociated with the disease. 

As the study's findings continue to 


| be disseminated, more and more pa- 
| tients are expected to get the mes 
| sage that they need to exercise tight 
| control over their blood glucose lev 


els if they wish to avoid long-term 
complications.

“It is important that pharmacists 
be up to date and learn as much as 
they can about the DCCT and [its] 
impact on long-term complications,” 
one manufacturer said. “People are 
going to rely on the pharmacist to 
counsel them and explain what tight 
control means.” 


Meters are not self-service 


Many customers also appreciate a 
pharmacist or technician that is 
willing to take the time and explain

how a blood glucose meter works. 

“We do customer training on 
every meter we sell,” Devine said. 
“The machines have gotten much 
easier to use, but people still need 
to have [them] explained and 
demonstrated.” 

The meter market is dominated by 
three major players: LifeScan, 
Boehringer Mannheim and Miles Di- 
agnostics. A fourth, Medisense, has 
begun to gain some ground and re- 
cently achieved a 10 percent market 
share. 

LifeScan continues to be the in 
dustry leader with its One Touch 
and One Touch II meters. One 
Touch test strips 
are the top-sell- 
ing item in the 
home diagnostics 
category with 
sales in food and 
drug stores of 
$116 million. 

“The DCCT 
findings will en 
sure continued 
growth in the di 
abetes category,” 
said LifeScan’s 
trade marketing 
manager Rick 
Taylor. “Im- 
proved education 
and awareness 
will help people 
recognize that 
the complica- 
tions of diabetes 
can be delayed or 
prevented with 
more frequent 
monitoring. In 
turn, an increase in meter and test 
strip sales will benefit both the 
pharmacy and the patient with dia- 
betes.” 

Within the past year, competition 
from lower cost, alternative test 
strips that can be used with Life- 
Scan meters have burst on the 
scene and accounted for sales of al- 
most $18 million through the 12 
months ended July 1994. Polymer 
Technology's First Choice strips and 
Can-Am Care’s Quick Check strips 


1994 


Strips 
81.9% 


1994 


DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, November 14, 1994 


are both the subject of patent in- 
fringement lawsuits brought by 
LifeScan. Court dates are currently 
set for early 1995. 

Behind LifeScan is Boehringer 
Mannheim. Its Accu-Chek family of 
products are familiar to many cus 
tomers and its ChemStrip bG, Trac 
er bG and Easy test strips account 
for food and drug sales of more than 
$70 million. Boehringer Mannheim 
also has a new meter on the market 
called The Advantage. 

Another significant player in the 
category is Miles Diagnostics with 
its Glocometer meters and Glucofilm 
and Glucostix test strips with sales 
approaching $30 million. Miles also 
introduced a new meter called the 
Encore this year. 

Occupying fourth place in the 
category is Medisense. The compa 
ny manufacturers the Companion 2 
and Exactech meter and both come 
in pen and card models. The me 
ters are smaller than the other 
leading players and many patients 
enjoy the portability and Medi 
sense’s individually wrapped test 
strips, according to product manag 
er and diabetes specialist Terri 
Schmidt. FTP

new distributor

| but it will no longer be distributed |

Drug wholesalers in ‘94: 
An industry on track for growth 


by Ken Rankin 


ven if you're on the 
right track, sooner 
or later you'll get 
| run over if you don't keep 
moving. Will Rogers wasn't 
| talking about wholesale 
| druggists when made that 
| observation 50 years ago, 
| but it’s a warning that ap- 
| plies to the industry 
| nonetheless. 
| Today's drug whole- 
| salers are indeed on track 
| to provide higher levels of 
service to retail pharmacy, 
| to accept new roles in the 
| drug delivery system and 
| to set new sales records in 
| the process. 
At the same time, the 
| wholesale drug industry is 
| beginning to reap rewards 
| from its long-term commit- 
| ment to productivity-en- 
hancing systems and tech- 
nology. And the efficiencies 
that flow from wholesaler 
investments in these areas 
| will help to hold down 
| health care costs for every- 
| one. 
But there’s danger just 
| around the curve. Whole- 
| sale industry profits are 
under mounting pressure 
due to shrinking margins 
| on new pharmaceutical 
| products and growing de- 
mands from managed 
care providers who want 
more for less from distrib- 
utors. 

The slowdown in phar- 
maceutical price inflation 
has added to the indus- 
try’s problems by making 
it more difficult for whole- 
salers to maintain mar-

Pricing crossfire 


But in addition to these 
economic troubles, whole 
sale druggists are now 
finding themselves caught 
in the crossfire as retail 
pharmacy groups and 
drug manufacturers 
square off for a shooting 
war over pharmaceutical 
industry pricing practices. 

Already, several major 
wholesalers have been 
named as co-conspirators 
in antitrust lawsuits filed 
by independent druggists 
to challenge the “discrimi 
natory pricing” schemes 
employed by many manu 
facturers. At the heart of 
those actions: wholesaler 
“chargebacks” which retail 
pharmacy leaders say 
make it easier for drug 
makers to pass on discrim 
inatory discounts to HMOs, 
mail-order providers and 
other managed care phar 
macy competitors. 

This battle isn’t con 
fined to the courtroom, 
however. State legisla 
tures across the country 
are under pressure to en 
act statutory restrictions 
on multi-tier pricing by 
pharmaceutical manufac 
turers. Both NARD and 
NACDS have been aggres 
sively lobbying for laws to 
eliminate discriminatory 
price discounts by manu 
facturers, and some of the 
state bills that emerged 
this year would create un

The nation’s top wholesalers 


McKesson Drug Co. had a 
new leader at its helm this 
past year. Jim Smith, former- 
ly senior vice president of 


Avnet Inc., the distributor of 


electrical and computer com- 
ponents, replaced Tom Si 
mone as 
McKesson Drug Co. 

Under Smith's direction, 
customer service continued 
to be a high priority at 
McKesson. “There's always a 
tendency to reorganize the 
business. We are really reor- 


| ganizing the workload so that 


people can do their jobs right 
and be more responsive to 
customer needs,” said Smith. 
Smith said that customers 
also sensed an increased re- 


president of 


sponsiveness from McKesson. 

To that end, the wholesaler 
has reorganized to redivide 
the country into three mar- 
kets, rather than two. The 
three areas, headed by three 
executive vice presidents, will 
have “a better span of deci- 
sion making and bring opera- 
tions closer to the field,” ac- 
cording to Smith. 

McKesson’'s focus on tech- 
nology has led to an increase 
in efficiency. The wholesaler’s 
Acumax Plus, a bar code 
scanning system, has en- 
hanced the quality of orders 
and helped McKesson achieve 
a zero mis-pick rate at some 
locations. 

The wholesaler closed its 
Kansas City DC; that market 
will now be serviced from the 
St. Louis facility. McKesson 


also consolidated its service 
merchandise operations into 
a single organization called 
Millbrook Distribution Ser 
vices. The new name is in 
tended to differentiate the 
service merchandise arm of 
McKesson from the drug dis 
tribution business. 

McKesson was also actively 
involved in telemarketing in 
1994. The wholesaler ran tele 
marketing programs with spe 
cific suppliers which allowed 
manufacturers to provide re 
tailers with information or sell 
product directly to the retailer. 

The wholesaler also signed 
an agreement to sell its PCS 
Health Systems,Inc. prescrip 
tion benefit management ser 
vices unit to Eli Lilly for $4 
billion. That sale is still pend 
ing FTC approval.

The nation’s top wholesalers 


#3 Cardinal 


Cardinal followed up last 
year’s acquisition of Whitmire 
with the buyout of Humiston- 
Keeling and Behrens Inc. to 
become one of the nation's 
dominant wholesalers. With 
annual sales this year of $8.6 
billion, Cardinal now ranks as 
the third largest wholesaler. 

Its Leader Drug voluntary 
program continues to grow and 
Cardinal today serves more 
than 13,000 retail customers 
and operates 40 distribution 
centers. The wholesaler offers a 
300-SKU private label line, a 


home health care program, in- 
teractive health information 
kiosks, a state-of-the-art phar- 
macy computer management 
system and its Cardinal Source 
generics program. 

Executive vp and cfo David 
Bearman expects Cardinal's 
advanced pharmacy manage- 
ment informational systems to 
attract new customers. The 
systems provide purchasing, 
inventory and management 
capabilities to 3,300 locations. 
That number is expected to 
grow to 4,000 in early '95. 

Cardinal is also the na- 
tion’s second largest pharma- 
ceutical repackager. 


#4 FoxMeyer 


In 1994, FoxMeyer contin- 
ued to improve its systems 
and distribution capabilities 
and introduce innovative new 
electronic services, marketing 
programs and niche busi- 
nesses. Last February, the 
wholesaler introduced its 
Phase II upgrade of QuestRx, 
its on-line pharmacy adjudi- 
cation system. The move dra- 
matically increased the sys- 
tem's capacity and efficiency. 

In other technology devel- 
opments, FoxMeyer developed 
an auto-substitution system 
to fill equivalent prescription 
products during supplier 
shortages and an autoreplen- 
ishement program to auto- 
mate the re-ordering process. 
In its purchasing area, 
FoxMeyer replaced cumber- 
some, paper-driven reporting 
procedures with an electron- 
ic, exception-only reporting 
system that focuses on key 
purchasing indices. 

In April, the wholesaler 
rolled out FoxCare Network, a 
system which _ provides 
FoxMeyer's pharmacy cus- 
tomers with greater access to 
national and local managed 
care plans through the cre- 
ation of a network of pharma- 
cies. FoxMeyer’s newest pro- 


gram, Diabetes Life Center, | 


will provide FoxMeyer's net- 
work of pharmacy customers 
with more information on dia- 
betes so that they can become 
more active caregivers for 
their customers with diabetes. 

FoxMeyer also_ broke 
ground on its 339,000- 
square-foot state-of-the-art 
mid-Ohio DC. The new DC is 
being billed as the most tech- 
nologically advanced, highest 
volume distribution center in 
the pharmaceutical industry. 

In 1994, FoxMeyer ac- 
quired Scrip Card Enterprises 
Inc., a Salt Lake City-based 
prescription benefits manage- 
ment company, signed a $4 
billion to $5 billion agreement 
with University Hospital Con- 
sortium, and won a $300 mil- 
lion to $350 million contract 
with BJC Health Systems. 
The new contracts are expect- 
ed to give FoxMeyer a greater 
percentage of institutional 
business. FoxMeyer does 33 
percent of its business with 
independents, 32 percent 
with chains, 25 percent with 
hospitals and alternate care 
sites and the balance with 
mail order. 

In October, FoxMeyer be- 
came a wholly-owned sub- 
sidiary of National Intergroup 
and changed its name to 
FoxMeyer Health Corporation.

Margins under ‘he gun 


Despite last’ year's 
strong sales growth and 
the prospect of more of the 
same in ‘94, the nation’s 
wholesale drug distribu- 
tors remain under heavy 
pressure to trim margins 
in today’s hotly-competi- 
tive pharmaceutical mar- 
ketplace. 

The NWDA’s Operating 
Survey confirms that in- 
dustrywide gross margins 
fell to a new all-time low 
last-year: 5.82 percent of 
sales—down from 6.5 per- 
cent in 1992. Shrinking 
margins aren't anything 


Wholesalers’ operating expenses asa 


sales a decade ago—from 
4.22 percent in 1992 to 
4.05 percent last year. 

But the wholesale drug 
industry's success in trim- 
ming costs while building 
gross profit dollars wasn't 
enough to prevent signifi- 
cant bottom-line erosion 
for many firms. After aver 
aging between 2.2 percent 
of sales and 2.9 percent 
for the past decade, total 
operating income among 
the nation’s wholesale 
druggists plunged to 1.78 
percent in 1993, according 
to NWDA's latest survey.

Wholesalers: On track 


| Continued from page 32 


Cardinal, while still digesting 


| last year’s massive acquisition of 
| Whitmire, added both Humiston- 
| Keeling and Behrens to solidify its 
| position as the nation’s third 
| largest drug wholesaler. 


Neuman Distributors leapfrogged 
into the industry's top 10 via its ac- 
quisition of OCP America. Other key 
mergers still in the works include 
Bindley Western's proposed pur- 
chase of Kendall and Walker Drug’s 
planned marriage with Albers. FTP 


AmeriSource, formerly Alco Health 
Services, continued its strong growth 
during 1994 and also completed a cor- 
porate identity change reflective of its 
nationwide reach. 

“Our new name declares our strategic 
intent to be the premier national phar- 
| maceutical wholesaler and major source 
of products and programs for America’s 
pharmacies,” said chairman and chief 
executive officer John McNamara. 

McNamara also believes the change 
will ultimately help eliminate confusion 
that has arisen over the years as the 


|| company grew rapidly and acquired 


many new divisions. Customer loyalty 

| was ensured by retaining the names of 

| the acquired businesses, but a single 
| corporate identity was needed. 

“The AmeriSource identity eliminates 

| any confusion that may have existed in 


The nation’s top wholesalers 


the past,” McNamara said. The name 
change did not affect the wholesaler's 
corporate management team or offered 
services such as the ECHO on-line in- 
ventory management system, Family 
Pharmacy merchandising programs or 
the pharmaceutical Re-Pax program. In 
addition, the Malvern, Pennsylvania- 
based company this year expanded its 
private label product offerings, devel- 
oped a cable television advertising pro- 
gram and offered contract pricing on 
products from national brand and 
generic suppliers. 

Those programs have helped 
AmeriSource double its sales since 
1988 and today it ranks as the nation's 
fifth largest wholesaler. AmeriSource 
currently operates 16 distribution cen- 
ters in 49 states and serves 18,000 
pharmacy customers. Its customer mix 
is comprised of 35 percent independent 
pharmacies, 21 percent chain stores 
and 44 percent hospitals. 


1 #6 Bindley Western 


It was a busy year for the nation’s 
sixth largest wholesaler. Indianapolis- 
based Bindley Western acquired 
Kendall Drug Company (fiscal 1994 
| sales of $110 million), opened a new 
warehouse, offered new technological 
sophistication to retail accounts and 
formed a new wholly owned subsidiary. 

“We are doing everything we can to 
find niche opportunities and new busi- 
nesses that will help us stay competi- 
tive,” said chairman and chief executive 
officer William Bindley. 

To further gain operating efficiency 
Bindley completed the consolidation of 
three warehouses in Stamford, Conn., 
into one 200,000-square-foot facility lo- 
cated in Orange, Conn. 

This year also saw Bindley Western 
complete the upgrade of technology 
systems available to its First Choice re- 


tailers. Electronic data interchange and 
automatic replenishment capabilities 
are now fully available. The wholesaler 
is also diversifying its product mix and 
looking for new areas to offset margin 
pressures on pharmaceuticals. 

Priority Healthcare Corp. is the 
wholesaler’s new subsidiary that was 
created to develop opportunities 
through health care reform in both the 
supplier and provider markets. 

“With today’s emphasis on outcome 
monitoring and disease management, 
Priority Healthcare will be in an ideal po- 
sition to capture patient specific informa- 
tion and create valuable disease state 
data bases,” said president Michael Vis- 
nich. “As these are developed, we hope to 
establish partnering arrangements with 
pharmaceutical and managed care com- 
panies to utilize the data in evaluating 
risk sharing and capitation programs.” 

Bindley Western operates 12 DCs 
and serves customers in 40 states.

Choosing a franchise or co-op ‘chain’ 


Continued from page 37 
novations in all major franchise and 
co-op groups. 


Private label lines 


Two of the largest franchisors, Medi 
cine Shoppe and Medicap, are in 
apothecary format. Health Mart, spon 
sored by wholesaler FoxMeyer, is a 
full-line program and some of its stores 
are large, high-volume locations. 


All formats offer a private label 
line, primarily in OTCs, as well as a 
generic drug program. Medicap and 
Medicine Shoppe each offer around 
300 SKUs. The apothecary-format 
franchisors are more specialized, 
whereas a full-line franchise like 
Health Mart offers HBA and even 
some private label GM, including 
film, in its 400-SKU line. 

The Medicine Shoppe and Medicap

private label lines both reflect the 
top brands in each OTC category, 
and include only health aids and 
not beauty aids. 


Selling quality 


In today’s competitive market 
place, the franchisor wants his 
“chain” to offer managed-care con- 
tract-worthy qualities. These in- 
clude a geographically desirable 
map (good coverage in a region or 
in several states, for example), and 
this is one reason a franchisor like 
Medicap is offering incentives (like 
a 50 percent discount) for a pioneer 
where there are no other examples 
of the franchise name. 

The differences between the 
“menu” of options and mandatory 
or strongly recommended programs 
are narrowing. In a managed-care 
environment, the need for quality 
assurance suggests that there will 
be more mandatory programs in 
the future, to assure pharmacy 
benefit managers and other third- 
party entities that the “chain” will 
deliver uniform pharmacy care to 
improve the outcomes and control 
the costs of patient care. 


Co-ops on the move 


What started as buying coopera 
tives years ago has evolved into a 
number of full-service networks for 
independent drug stores. Coopera 
tives—which give rebates on the co 
op’s profits to members—offer a 
range of marketing and operational 
Support and many have programs to 
pursue third-party prescription 
business for member stores 

In total, there are more than two 
dozen co-ops and most belong to the 
25-member Federation of Pharmacy 
Networks, which represents the na 
tion’s primary buying groups and 
around 10,000 pharmacies. 

The largest is 800-member United 
Drug based in Phoenix. Established 
in 1977 as American Associated 
Druggists, United has grown rapidly 
in the past few years, adding 328 
stores to its ranks in 1993 alone. It 
now has around 800 co-op members. 


38 


it offers its mem 
bers a full range of 
services from vol- 
ume buying and ad- 
vertising/ marketing 
to a recent innova- 
tion, an in-store 
electronic advertis- 
ing program for na- 
tional advertisers, 
which adds extra in- 
come for participat- 
ing member stores. 

Another major 
player is the 500 
member national affiliation of Legend 
Pharmacies, with regional groups in 
the Southwest, Northeast (Philadel- 
phia) and Southeast (Louisiana). 

One of the largest of the Legend 
regional groups is Legend Pharma- 
cies Southwest, founded in 1982 by 
19 independent pharmacy owners. 


Phoenix-based United Drug was established in 1977 and is 
the largest co-op with 800 members. 


Today it has 275 owner/members in 
four states (Texas, Arkansas, Okla- 
homa and New Mexico), employs a 
full-time staff and works closely 
with other Legend co-ops. These are 
located in the Northeast and 
Louisiana for a combined total of 
around 500 members in 11 states.

and Drug Administration’s plan for new regulations resurrecting pa- 
tient package inserts

Canadian drug There’s a retail pharmacy revolution brewing north of the border, 


where the Canadian Association of Chain Drug Stores (CACDS) has 


chains seek links severed ties with the Canadian Pharmaceutical Association—an um- 


brella group representing various segments of the nation’s drug dis- 

with independents tribution industry. At the same time, CACDS—which represents 
over a dozen chains operating some 2,000 pharmacies throughout 
Canada—will open its membership to the country’s independent 
drug stores. The result: a super retail pharmacy organization in 
Canada that could fuel speculation about a future NARD-NACDS 
merger in the U.S.

o DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, JANUARY 1995 


by Ken Rankin 


he generic drug industry 
caught fire last year, and the 
demand for cost-effective 
generic pharmaceuticals fig- 
ures to remain hot throughout 1995. 

Tagamet®, Cardizem CD®, 
Clozaril® and other big-money brand 
name drugs came off patent last 
year, and at least 40 more leading 
pharmaceuticals will lose patent pro- 
tection over the next five years. The 
result: many top marketers of gener- 
ic drugs posted double- or even 
triple-digit sales gains last year, and 
all indications point to another 
strong growth surge in 1995. 

But some observers contend that 
the generic drug industry’s growth 
rate can’t be sustained at its current 
blistering pace. Although the de- 
mand for generics will continue to 
rise well into the next century, an ex- 


pected slowdown in the number of 


blockbuster brand name drugs com- 
ing off patent over the next few years 
could cool the industry’s growth con- 
siderably. 

A recent study by industry analyst 
David Saks pegged the market value 
of the brand-name pharmaceuticals 
whose patents expired in 1993 and 
1994 at more than $9 billion. In con- 
trast, Saks’ analysis indicates that 
the value of drugs coming off patent 
from 1995 until the turn of the cen- 
tury will average only $1.6 billion 
annually. Moreover, there are indica- 
tions that manufacturers of pioneer 
pharmaceuticals are finding ways to 
maintain a significant share of the 
market even after their products lose 
patent protection. 

When Tagamet’s patent expired 
last year, for example, SmithKline 
Beecham began marketing its own 
generic version. Similarly, several 
other top selling pharmaceuticals 
that went off patent over the past 
two years—including Syntex’ 
Naprosyn®, Hoechst-Roussel’s Dia- 
beta®, and Upjohn’s Xanax® and 


alcion®—are now bei arkete 
Halcion® re now being marketed 
generically by their original manu- 
facturers. 


Managed care mixed blessing 


Although the generic drug indus- 


try is expecting the on-going push for 


health care reform to create a 
stronger demand for more cost-effec- 
tive pharmaceuticals, the emphasis 
on shifting to a managed care ap- 


en COVER STORY meen

generic drug industry 


a series of developments combining 
to increase demand for these prod- 
ucts over the next few years. Citing 
the reunification of Germany, in- 
creasingly favorable monetary ex- 
change rates and the possibility of 
new legislation to encourage generic 
prescribing in key countries, re- 
searchers at Frost & Sullivan are 
predicting that the Western Euro- 
pean market for generics will rise to

proach could also work to the disad-

With institutions, HMOs and other 
managed care providers often able to 
buy brand-name pharmaceuticals at 
deep discounts, generic manufacturers 
have traditionally had difficulty gain- 
ing a stronghold in these markets. As 
these trends threaten to limit future 
growth prospects for generics in the 
U.S., an increasing number of manu- 
facturers are looking to expand inter- 
nationally into new and potentially ex- 
plosive markets throughout the world. 


Looking toward Europe 


In Western Europe, where generic 
drug sales reached an estimated $4.9 
billion in 1993, industry analysts see 


$6.6 billion by 1998. 

Other experts, however, contend 
that the prospects for generic drugs 
in European Community (EC) na- 
tions would be even brighter but for 
a pro-brand-name pharmaceutical 
bias in many of these countries. 

Klaus Lichtenberger, executive 
vice president at Germany’s Ratio- 
pharm GmbH & Co., contends that 
the current emphasis in the EC is on 
promoting growth among European 
research-based pharmaceutical com- 
panies, often at the expense of 
generics. Southern European coun- 
tries such as Italy, France, Spain 
and Portugal have erected particu- 
larly stiff legislative and regulatory

barriers to the use of generics, he 
told the recent Global Generic Con- 
ference in New York. 

Lichtenberger maintains that “be- 
fore generics will get a real chance in 
these countries, legal conditions will 
have to be changed, and the few ex- 
isting generic companies have to do a 
lot of work to convince doctors, phar- 


macists and patients of the benefit of 


generics.” 

In contrast, strong interest in cost- 
saving generic pharmaceuticals 
among the newly emerging democra- 
cies of Eastern Europe and the former 
Soviet Union is creating opportunities 
for some of the top players in the U.S. 
generic drug market. Pennsylvania- 
based Lemmon recently joined with 
its Israeli parent, Teva Pharmaceuti- 
cal Industries, to form a new partner- 
ship with a Russian company to pro- 
duce generics in that country. Teva 
| has also set up pharmaceutical mar- 
keting organizations in Hungary and 
Romania, and has launched “an in- 
tense campaign to register our prod- 
ucts in several countries in Western 
and Eastern Europe, as well as in the 
former Soviet Republics,” a company 
spokesperson said. 

Novopharm is another leading 
North American generic producer 
with a stake in the Eastern Euro- 
pean market via its recent acquisi- 
tion of tituman Pharma/Human 
Serum of Budapest, Hungary. 

Other generic industry leaders are 
looking to cash in on growth opportu- 
nities in different corners of the in- 
ternational marketplace. Copley 
Pharmaceutical recently estab- 
lished a generic marketing foothold 
in China, Teva’s future growth plans 
include establishing a meaningful 
market presence in Argentina and 
IVAX appears poised for global ex- 
pansion. Through its Goldline and 
Norton Healthcare subsidiaries, Mia- 
mi-based IVAX already markets an 
extensive line of generics in the UK 
and other countries, and these inter- 
national marketing efforts may ex- 
pand in the wake of the company’s 
merger with Zenith Labs. 

According to Zenith chief executive 
officer John Klein, the “global manu- 
facturing, marketing and distribu- 
tion network” achieved through the 
merger will open “great opportunities 
for our combined company.”

Company profiles 


Beyond looking overseas, here’s what some of the leading 
and most interesting generic companies are doing to stay 


competitive: 


No holds ‘Barr’ed for future growth 


he past year has 

produced a reversal 
of fortunes for Barr 
Laboratories—a com 
pany beset with finan 
cial as well as regulato 
ry difficulties over the 
last several years. Barr’s successes 
in 1994 include a return to opera- 
tional profitability, strong demand 
for the company’s newly launched 
tamoxifen citrate and a long-await 
ed determination by the Food and 
Drug Administration (FDA) that 
Barr's facilities are in compliance 
with the agency's Current Good 
Manufacturing Practice regulations. 

More importantly, perhaps, offi- 
cials at Barr were able to put an 
end to the company’s bitter conflict 
with the FDA over manufacturing 
practices at the firm’s Northvale, 


N.J., and Pomona, N.Y., facilities. 
In February 1993 a federal judge 
sided with the FDA and temporari- 
ly suspended 24 Barr products. 
The company responded with a 
countersuit against the FDA and 
has since been able to reintroduce 
only about half of the suspended 
drugs. The settlement of those le- 
gal actions, which was announced 
late last year, “allows Barr to 
strengthen our position as a lead- 
ing provider of generic pharmaceu- 
ticals by permitting us to reintro- 
duce many formerly marketed 
medicines that continue to offer 
significant benefits for our cus- 
tomers,” company president Bruce 
L. Downey said. 
But in addition to the reintro- 
ductions of those suspended

Company profiles 


Business fueled by Lemmons pipeline 


flurry of important 
ew ANDA ap- 
provals have helped 
spark a surge of higher 
generic drug sales for 
eee Kulpsville, Pa-based 
Lemmon. Sales rose 
more than 76 percent to $185 million 
during 1993, with half of that total 
coming from products approved for 
marketing in 1992 and 1993. 

The growing demand for Lem- 
mon’s generic pharmaceuticals both 
in the U.S. and abroad was a major 
factor in boosting sales at the compa- 
ny’s corporate parent, Teva Pharma- 
ceutical Industries, to new record 
levels. According to officials at Teva, 
Israel’s leading pharmaceutical man- 
ufacturer, the company’s consolidat- 
ed sales for 1993 topped the half bil- 
lion dollar mark—a 27 percent 
increase over the previous year— 
while earnings climbed 81 percent to 
$57.5 million. The “dramatic growth” 
experienced by Lemmon “accounted 
for most of Teva’s increased sales 
and income,” Teva president Eli 
Hurvitz acknowledged. 

Lemmon has been in the generic 
pharmaceutical business for half a 


hdicsal 


century, and currently markets a 
broad range of drugs in 47 different 
product groups. The company’s top 
sellers include gemfibrozil tablets 
(equivalent to Parke Davis’ Lopid), 
naproxen (the generic version of Syn- 
tex’s Naprosyn), naproxen sodium 
tablets (the equivalent of Syntex’s 
Anaprox) and diltiazem extended-re- 
lease capsules (the generic equiva- 
lent of Marion Merrell Dow’s 
Cardizem SR). 

Lemmon also markets generic 
theophylline extended-release cap- 
sules, and butalbital, acetaminophen 
and caffeine tablets. Other recent 
product introductions that have 
helped to increase the company’s 
market share include the first gener- 
ic carbidopa and levodopa tablets. 

The emphasis on growth through 
new product introductions continues 
at Lemmon, as the company obtained 
five new product approvals during 
1993, four of which came in the 
fourth quarter and are expected to 
have a positive impact on sales in 
1994 and 1995. According to Hurvitz, 
“we have a large number of products 
in the pipeline, and intend to submit 
further ANDAs for FDA approval.” 


No holds ‘Barr’ed for future growth 


Continued from page 14 

drugs, officials at the firm say the 
“normalization of relations with 
the FDA is expected to result in a 
stream of new product introduc- 
tions” to bolster Barr’s current line 
of 39 generics. Even before the 
settlement with the FDA, Barr was 
on track for a strong rebound as 
sales in the company's 1994 fiscal 
year rose 88 percent to $109 mil- 
lion. Early financial results for the 
current fiscal year show even 
stronger gains for Barr, with first 
quarter sales up 234 percent over 
the year before. 

“During the coming year, Barr 
intends to build on the momentum 
gained in 1994,” company spokes- 
man Charles Mayr said. “The com- 
pany’s efforts will include an ag- 
gressive research and development 
program (with expenditures up 50 
percent over 1994 levels), selective- 


ly returning older products to mar- 
ket and pursuing less traditional 
opportunities” for growth. Those 
opportunities include “collabora- 
tions” with other manufacturers as 
well as patent challenges, such as 
Barr's current efforts to invalidate 
the patents for Burroughs Well- 
come’s AZT and Miles’ Cipro. 

The company’s product line 
stretches across several key thera- 
peutic categories, including cardio- 
vascular agents, antibiotics, oncol- 
ogy products, analgesics and 
anti-infectives. 

In addition to tamoxifen citrate 
(which Barr distributes under an 
agreement with Zeneca), the com- 
pany’s top-selling generics include 
leucovorin calcium, cephalexin, 
erythromycin, tetracycline, dipyri- 
damole, amiloride, triamterene, 
isoniazid and methotrexate tablets 
in bottles and dose packs. 


Lemmon also offers a series of pro- 
grams tailored to the needs of com- 
munity pharmacists, including re- 
bates on pharmaceutical purchases, 
a “super savings” patient coupon pro- 
gram to assist consumers in selecting 
generic equivalents, and a series of 
patient education materials on 
Parkinson’s disease. 

Along with Teva, Lemmon is part- 
nering with a company that is plan- 
ning to produce and commercially 
market pharmaceuticals in Russia. 
Here in the U.S., officials at the par- 
ent company expect the continuing 
climate favoring health care reform 
to result in increasing demand for 
the company’s generics. 


DEY: No. 1 in 


ance its founding in 
the late 1970s, DEY 
Labs has evolved from a 
small provider of saline 
products to the coun- 
try’s largest manufac- 
turer of generic, unit- 
dose inhalation medications. Today, 
the company supplies inhalation so- 
lutions for asthma and bronchitis, 
chronic obstructive pulmonary dis- 
ease and pleurisy. Although hospi- 
tals have been DEY’s focus, the com- 
pany says the firm is also “a rapidly 
growing factor in the retail pharmacy 
and home care markets.” 

Over the years, DEY has also been 
able to claim a number of major 
“firsts” in the production of respirato- 
ry inhalations. DEY was the first to 
introduce sulfite-free isoetharine in- 
halation solutions in unit dose vials, 
the first to supply generic acetylcys- 
teine solution USP and the first to 
market cromolyn sodium inhalation 
USP in plastic unit dose vials. 

DEY’s production facilities are ca- 
pable of manufacturing millions of 
plastic unit dose vials each month. 
Over the long haul, officials at DEY 
say they are committed to growth 
through new product introductions. 
Indeed, they plan to launch “at least 
two new products per year through 
the year 2000 and beyond.”

DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, JANUARY 1995 


COVER STORY 


Company profiles 


Hoescht alliance stokes Copley growth 


At Copley Pharmaceuti- 
cal, the introduction of 
a flurry of new products 
has prompted a strong 
upswing in the compa- 
ny’s generic drug sales. 
And a new corporate al- 
liance with Hoechst figures to fuel 
further growth for Copley in the 
managed care arena. 

Canton, Mass.-based Copley en- 
tered the generic drug business in 
the early 1970s and currently mar- 
kets a line of 81 products. Although 
the company’s key area of strength is 
in timed-release drugs, the top sell- 
ers in Copley’s product line run the 
gamut from Procainamide ER tablets 
to Miconazole Vaginal Cream. 

It’s the new entries that are stir- 
ring much of the growth for Copley, 
however. Among the latest generics 
added to the company’s line or sub- 
ject to recent ANDA approvals: gly- 
buride tablets, guanabenz tablets, li- 
docaine jelly, quinidine sulfate 
tablets, hydrocortisone enema, hy- 
droxychloroquine tablets, clobetasol 
cream and ointment and metoprolol 


tablets. 

Due largely to the strong accep- 
tance of these new entries, Copley’s 
dollar sales climbed by 66 percent 
during the 1994 fiscal year to a 
record $86 million. Despite disrup- 
tions caused by several recent re- 
calls, the company’s quarterly results 
for the current year show continued 
solid gains. 


Changes promote market growth 


To keep pace with the growing de- 
mand for its generic line, Copley 
opened a new state-of-the-art 
168,000-square-foot addition to its 
Canton facility last year. 

In a move designed to position 
Copley for further growth in the 
generic market, the company sold 51 
percent of its outstanding shares to 
Hoechst-Celanese, a subsidiary of 
Hoechst AG. Under the arrange- 
ment, Copley received authority to 
market certain off-patent brand 
name drugs produced by Hoescht- 
Roussel Pharmaceuticals Inc., and is 
expected to benefit from the corpo- 
rate parent’s extensive managed-care 


sales force. 

According to company officials, the 
eventual goal is to create “a 100- 
product formulary defined by thera- 
peutic category for the HRPI/Copley 
Managed Health-Care sales force.” 

Additionally, they hope to capital- 
ize on “the vast opportunities pre- 
sented by Hoechst” in order to devel- 
op “active sourcing, alternative 
international manufacturing facili- 
ties and worldwide registration of 
Copley products.” Copley has already 
begun to expand into the internation- 
al generic pharmaceutical market, 
and just last June became the first 
U.S. generic manufacturer to estab- 
lish a base of operations in China. As 
part of a joint venture with a Chi- 
nese company, Copley plans to dis- 
tribute and eventually manufacture 
generic drugs in that country. 

Company officials say Copley’s 
eventual goal is to “focus on a continu- 
ous pipeline of new products, to bal- 
ance product selections” and to identi- 
fy “niche opportunities” to meet the 
demands of a constantly changing 
generic drug marketplace. 


Marketing gamble pays off for Novopharm 


The late 1980s, the 
darkest period in the 
history of the American 
generic drug industrv, 
may have seemed like a 
risky time for a Canadi- 
an pharmaceutical firm 
to establish a beachhead in the U.S. 
generic market. 

But if Novopharm Ltd.’s decision to 
enter the U.S. generic industry five 
years ago was an international mar- 
keting gamble, it’s one that is paying 
off in spades for the company today. 

Sales by Novopharm USA explod- 
ed by 250 percent to $85 million dur- 
ing the company’s 1994 fiscal year, 
and officials at the Schaumburg, IIl.- 
based generic house expect even 
faster growth in 1995. 

According to Novopharm USA 
president Bob Gunter, this year’s 
revenues are projected to rise 360 


as one of this country’s largest mar- 
keters of generic pharmaceuticals. 

Novopharm USA’s corporate par- 
ent, a fixture in the Canadian gener- 
ic drug industry for the past 30 
years, markets a line of more than 
250 pharmaceuticals in that country. 

In contrast, the U.S. subsidiary’s 
46-product generic line offers 
Novopharm ample opportunity for 
expansion. Indeed, during the past 
year alone, the company’s product 
mix has been increased to include 
such new big sellers as metoprolol, 
naproxen, naproxen sodium, dilti- 
azem, pindolol and atenolol. 

One of Novopharm’s most success- 
ful 1994 new product entries, cimeti- 
dine, is expected to generate first-year 
sales of $60 million for the company. 

Additionally, Novopharm USA has 
received tentative approva! to mar- 
ket ranitidine, a product which com- 


Over the longer run, Novopharm 
USA plans to file more than 50 new 
ANDAs with the Food and Drug Ad- 
ministration—a move that could 
quickly double the company’s exist- 
ing product base. 

Antibiotics are a key area of 
strength for Novopharm, and the com- 
pany’s present line includes a number 
of top selling generics, including 
amoxicillin and cephalexin. 

To keep pace with the U.S. demand 
for its generic drugs, Novopharm re- 
cently acquired Wilson, N.C.-based 
Granutec, Inc. and is developing U.S. 
production facilities at that location. 

Beyond North America, 
Novopharm has strengthened its in- 
ternational presence through a re- 
cent alliance with Wyeth-Ayerst’s 
newly formed European generic busi- 
ness—Pan-Efka. This strategic part- 
nership provides a global pipeline for

DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, JANUARY 1995 19 


COVER STORY 


Company profiles 


Mergers, aquisitions spark IVAX growth 


If there’s a company on 
a roll in the generic 
drug industry, it’s Mia- 
mi, Fla-based IVAX. 
Over the past four 
years the company’s 
sales have tripled, 
largely as a result of a series of major 
mergers and acquisitions that have 
strengthened IVAX’s market position 
both in the U.S. and abroad. 

In the U.S., retail pharmacy is 
most familiar with the company 
through its Goldline Laboratory sub- 
sidiary, which markets some 700 
generic prescription and OTC drugs 
in this country and abroad. The com- 
pany’s Norton Healthcare subsidiary, 
which is based in the United King- 
dom, produces another 140 generics 
in more than 250 dosage strengths. 


Strategic acquisitions 


In 1993, IVAX posted net revenues 
of $996 million and is poised for sig- 
nificant growth as a result of several 
more key acquisitions during the last 
year. The first of these took place in 
March when IVAX bought McGaw, 
Inc., a supplier of intravenous prod- 
ucts and services. That acquisition 
added more than $300 million to 
IVAX’s annual revenues, and some 
analysts predict it will help the com- 
pany expand at an annual rate of 40 
percent over the next two years. 

Next, IVAX targeted the Eastern 
European generic drug market with 
the purchase of a majority interest in 
Galena A.S.—a $50-million-per-year 
pharmaceutical maker based in the 
Czech Republic. 

Most recently, IVAX bit off a sig- 
nificant chunk of the U.S. generic in- 
dustry with the acquisition of Zenith 
Labs—a takeover, which at press 
time, was expected to be finalized by 
the end of last month. According to 
company officials, that merger “will 
create the world’s largest interna- 
tional generic pharmaceutical com- 
pany.” The combined 1993 net rev- 
enues of IVAX and Zenith from 
generic drugs was $570 million—75 
percent of which was derived from 
sales in the U.S. 

Together, the two companies cur- 
rently produce a total of 63 generic 
products in 145 dosage strengths for 


the U.S. market. Although IVAX is a 
diversified provider of health care 
products and services, about half of the 
company’s revenues flow from the sale 
of generic pharmaceuticals. 

In 1993, IVAX’s sales from phar- 
maceuticals rose nearly 30 percent to 
top the $500 million mark. The com- 
pany’s top seller is verapamil, a 
generic which produced more than 


Europe’s generic market 


$140 million of IVAX’s pharmaceuti- 
cal revenue in 1993. Last summer 
IVAX won FDA approval to market 
the first once-a-day sustained release 
verapamil product that is bioequiva- 
lent to Calan SR and Isoptin SR. 

In addition to drugs, IVAX mar- 
kets medical diagnostic items, spe- 
cialty chemicals and personal care 
products.

TE = COVER STORY ««—-—» 
Company profiles 


Merger expands Zenith product, service 


At presstime, officials 
at Zenith Laboratories 
were completing the fi- 
nal arrangements for 
the company’s merger 
with IVAX Corp. and— 
barring an eleventh- 
hour glitch—will begin operations 
this year as part of a wholly owned 
subsidiary of that firm. 

Under the game plan, Zenith will 
be integrated into the parent compa- 
ny’s U.S. Pharmaceutical Group, 
which will be able to offer pharma- 
cists “a greatly expanded selection” 
of generics, “as well as enhanced or- 
dering and distribution services.” 

In 1993, Zenith’s net sales hit a 
record $96 million, up more than 60 
percent from the previous year as net 
income ballooned to more than $18 
million—a performance that made 
the company an attractive takeover 
target for acquisition-hungry IVAX. 

For their part, officials at the 
Northdale, N.J.-based company at- 
tribute this strong growth rate to 
the high level of confidence in the 
company’s generic drugs among 
pharmacists. 

“Zenith’s commitment is to contin- 


uously provide the highest level of 
service and products to our cus- 
tomers,” said executive vice presi- 
dent Lenora C. Gavalas. 

Zenith Labs has been manufactur- 
ing generics for 38 years, and accord- 
ing to management officials, the com- 
pany is one of the few generic drug 
manufacturers “that are mature 
enough to meet the challenges of 
competition and price pressure.” 

Currently, Zenith markets a line 
of 40 products that includes among 
its top sellers nitrofurantoin macro- 
crystals—the generic equivalent of 
Macrodantin Capsules, an antibacte- 
rial agent for specific urinary tract 
infections. 

The company also recently re- 
ceived approval to market pindolol 
tablets (generic Visken) for the treat- 
ment of hypertension. Additionally, 
Zenith produces and markets a num- 
ber of oral solid-dosage products cov- 
ering a broad spectrum of clinical in- 
dications including hormonal, 
cardiovascular, infectious disease, 
gastrointestinal and central nervous 
system. 

Zenith is one generic manufac- 
turer that places a heavy emphasis 


on research and development 
(R&D) ctivities. The company in- 
vested $24 million in R&D activi- 
ties in the four years from 1990- 
1993, including a record $7.9 
million in 93, and spent another $4 
million on laboratory and piiot 
plant equipment and facilities. 


Quality at a low cost 


Currently, Zenith has 21 ANDAs 
covering 42 dosage strengths pend- 
ing with the Food and Drug Adminis- 
tration (FDA). “Zenith’s financial ob- 
jective is to remain the lowest cost 
producer of quality pharmaceutical 
products, enabling us to compete 
profitably in ali channels of distribu- 
tion,” says vice president/finance 
Richard H. Friedman. 

For the 1990s, the company’s goal 
“is to be the largest multi-source 
manufacturer with the broadest prod- 
uct line,” Zenith said. 

New generics now in the pipeline 
will increase the more than 200 AN- 
DAs currently owned by Zenith and 
help the company broaden into new 
therapeutic classes including cancer 
agents, anticoagulants and anti- 
Parkinson drugs.

COVER STORY 


Company profiles 


Rosemont boosts production, sales force 


Anticipating a series of 
new product approvals 
by the Food and Drug 
Administration (FDA) 
in 1995, Denver-based 
Rosemont Pharmaceu- 
tical has already 
mapped out plans to expand its 
generic production capacity and in- 
crease its sales force during the com- 
ing year. 

The company, which last year 
changed its name from Pharmaceuti- 
cal Basics Inc., is a subsidiary of Akzo 
Nobel—a diversified, $10.5 billion a 
year multi-national producer of chem- 
icals, fibers and pharmaceuticals 
headquartered in the Netherlands. 

Although Akzo does not report 
revenues by Rosemont separately, of- 
ficials at the eight-year-old generic 
company said dollar sales from its 
drug products rose 31 percent in 
1994. For the current (1995) fiscal 
year, Rosemont expects to see its 
generic drug sales to increase by an- 
other 25 percent. 

New product introductions during 
1994 include piroxicam and cimeti- 


dine—already one of Rosemont’s top 
sellers. Other leading products in 
the company’s generic line include 
medroxyprogesterone and chlorpro- 
mazine. 

Although the Rosemont name is 
new, pharmaceuticals have been pro- 
duced in the company’s Denver plant 
since 1933, when it was known as 


Western Research Laboratories. 

Since being acquired by Akzo No- 
bel in 1986, the company has made 
substantial investments in equip- 
ment and human resources to en- 
hance product quality. According to 
officials at Rosemont, one of every 
five company employees works in a 
quality control capacity.

How low can third-party prescrip- 
tion reimbursement go? Diversified 
Pharmaceutical Services (DPS) may 
have discovered the bottom of the bar- 
rel this fall when it announced plans 
to slash pharmacy reimbursement for 
prescriptions dispensed to Westing- 
house employees. 

Under the plan, the already-low 
AWP minus 10 percent plus a $2.50 
fee would be cut to AWP less 7 per- 
cent with no dispensing fee. The 
new reimbursement structure had 
been slated to take effect late last 
year, but so few pharmacists were 
willing to participate that DPS can- 
celed the reduction. “For years, com- 
munity retail pharmacists have 
been coerced into accepting ‘take it 
or leave it’ contracts offering unrea- 


Charles West. “It appears pharma- 
cists, individually, have said 
‘enough is enough.” 


Humphrey blasts 
PBM takeovers 
by drug makers 


Minnesota Attorney General Hu- 
bert H. Humphrey III is warning 
that the recent rash of mergers be- 
tween brand name drug manufac- 
turers and pharmaceutical benefit 
management (PBMs) companies 
could “substantially lessen competi- 
tion” in the third-party drug mar- 
ketplace. Citing reports that Merck 
uses Medco mail-order pharmacists 
“to persuade doctors to switch 
75,000 prescriptions a month to 
those drugs favored by Medco,” 
Humphrey criticized these counter- 


Adding to the attorney general’s 
concern is the reported failure of the 
telemarketers to inform physicians 
that they are Merck/Medco employ- 
ees. In a recent letter to federal an- 
titrusters, Humphrey also warned 
that the vertical integration of phar- 
maceutical manufacturers into 
PBMs could lead to the “exploita- 
tion” of the generic drug market. 
“Merck, SmithKline and Lilly can be 
expected to treat their respective 
PBM customer bases, totaling 91 
million Americans, as captive mar- 
kets for their own generic products, 
which they may even price at a pre- 
mium to other generics,” he 
charged. FTP

Analgesics decisions abound, 
expanding pharmacists’ role 


by Mike Troy 


fter enjoying years of stability 
and steady sales growth, the 
TC analgesics category has 
lately encountered turbulence and 
accelerated growth. The past year 
saw the introduction of new brands 
and active ingredients, advertising 
campaigns that target products to 
specific conditions and that attempt 
to educate consumers on the benefits 
of various active ingredients. The 
state of the category is such that con- 
sumers seeking pain relief have more 
OTC options than ever before. 

With all this activity, the potential 
increases for consumer confusion, 
and the pharmacist’s role has grown 
accordingly as a patient counselor 
and product recommender. 

The most significant development 
in the category was last year’s intro- 
duction of Aleve. A strong prescrip- 
tion heritage and widespread publici- 
ty of the naproxen sodium product 
from Procter & Gamble/Syntex gen- 
erated strong interest, as it was the 
first new OTC analgesic ingredient 
since ibuprofen was switched in 
1984. Advertising for the product has 
focused on its duration of relief, 
which can be up to 12 hours. 

“In controlled clinical testing, 
Aleve’s duration of pain relief was 
shown to be longer than that of Extra- 
Strength Tylenol. Aleve was also 
shown in clinical trials to be at least 
as effective as Advil, with evidence of 
longer duration of pain relief in some

Acetaminophen remains the most pop-

When consumers reach for pain relievers 


When it comes to types of pain, headaches are the overwhelming reason 
most people turn to non-prescription pain relievers. That’s according to 
New York-based Simmons’ Market Research’s annual survey of media and 
markets. Seventy-four percent of U.S. adults surveyed said they use pain 
relievers for headaches, according to Simmons. Coming in a distant second 
at 38.5 percent was cold, flu or fever pain. Muscle or body aches was cited 
as the reason for using a pain reliever by 27.3 percent of those responding. 
Backaches (21.8 percent), menstrual pain (17 percent), sinus/allergies (14 
percent) and arthritis (12.5 percent) were other reasons survey respon- 
dents gave for using pain relievers. Patients or pharmacists who would like 
more information or wish to receive a monthly newsletter on headache 
pain, can contact either the National Headache Foundation at (800) 843- 
2256 or the American Council on Headache Education at (800) 255-2243. 


Consumers continue to turn to drug 
stores for their pain reliever needs, 
and the trade class maintains a slight 
market share edge over food stores. 


Advil, is involved in a legal battle 
over Aleve’s advertising claims. At 
press time, spokespeople for both 
sides said a ruling is expected soon. 
Whitehall-Robins contends there is 
no data to show that Aleve provides 
longer lasting pain relief than Advil, 
and that Aleve’s recommended dosing 
schedule of one pill every 8 to 12 
hours is related to the product’s safe- 
ty, not its duration of effectiveness. 
“After more than 10 years on the 
shelves, Advil remains a leader in the 
OTC analgesic market with an excel- 
lent record of safety and efficacy. No 
other OTC analgesic has been proven 
to be more effective or to last longer 
than Advil,” said Terrence Stecz, pres- 
ident of Whitehall-Robins. A 
spokesman for P&G said the company 
is “confident of being able to retain its 
advertising claims regarding Aleve.” 


Ibuprofen still popular 


Despite the introduction of an 
OTC naproxen sodium product, 
ibuprofen as an active ingredient 
continues to be popular. Ibuprofen 
products account for 25.1 percent of 
analgesic sales, which is about a 6 
percent increase from a year ago. 

Acetaminophen-containing prod- 
ucts’ share of the market has declined 
somewhat, but still remains a domi- 
nant 45.2 percent. Even though the

Re VIEWPOINT §«—- I 


Consumers get label supplement 


ontrary to reports elsewhere, 

health care reform legislation 

was approved by the 103rd 
Congress. It was signed into law by 
President Bill Clinton on Oct. 25, 
1994 and will affect approximately 
120 million people. This new health 
care law doesn’t deal with universal 
coverage, any willing provider provi- 
sions or equal access to pharmaceuti- 
cal discounts, yet its effects are more 
far-reaching than any Democratic or 
Republican proposal dealing with 
those issues. 

The public’s ability to educate 
themselves and make informed 
choices about nutritional supple- 
ments is the focus of the health care 
reform legislation known as the Di- 
etary Supplement and Health 
Education Act of 1994. It is prop- 
erly being hailed as landmark legis- 
lation, especially at a time when 
consumer demand for these products 
is growing. 

This legislation will benefit retail- 
ers, manufacturers and pharmacists. 
The biggest winners, however, are the 
ever increasing numbers of consumers 
who are turning to nutritional supple- 
ments (primarily vitamins and herbs) 
because of the products’ disease fight- 
ing and health improving benefits 

Consumers win because the new 


law allows manufac- 
turers to provide edu- 
cational literature 
about product benefits. 
No longer will con- 
sumers have to won- 
der what garlic is for, 
or rely on newspaper or 
magazine articles to 
educate themselves on 
free radical-fighting 
anti-oxidants. Under 
the new law, manufac- 


turers will be able to 
provide retailers with

garlic example. 
manufacturers 
can talk about 
garlic s choles- 
terol-lowering 
abilities, but can’t 
go the additional 
step and make 
the point that 
lower cholesterol 
levels can reduce 
the risk of heart 
disease 

The net effect of 
this new law is 


third-party literature 
as long as it doesn't 
promote their brand, isn’t false or mis- 
leading and, if displayed, is separate 
from dietary supplement products 


Compare labels 


The other big consumer benefit 
that is long overdue relates to label- 
ing and will enable consumers to 
compare brands. Manufacturers 
must include on the label the name, 
quantity and weight of each ingredi- 
ent, and the identity of any part of 
the plant from which a botanical in- 
gredient is derived. Labels can alse 
describe a dietary supplement’s 
proven effect on the structure or 
function of the body. To revisit the 


that an informa- 
tion-starved public 
will be better informed about nutri- 
tional products. Through provisions in 
the law, consumers will be able to ed- 
ucate themselves about which prod- 
ucts to use and, therefore, exert 
greater control over their health 

The bottom line for product manu- 
facturers and drug store retailers 
many of whom are devoting more 
space to supplements and have al- 
ready seen healthy sales growth —will 
be even stronger gains in 1995. And 
this health care reform legislation 
should be welcomed by any pharma- 
cist who has ever groped for an an- 
swer to a customer's question about 
nutritional supplements FIP

popularity of acetaminophen as an in-

Proceeds fight arthritis 


Another new brand that retailers 
and pharmacists report is selling well 
is McNeil’s Arthritis Foundation 
brand. Products in the line contain 
aspirin, acetaminophen or ibuprofen, 
and point-of-purchase materials let 
would-be consumers know that $1 
million a year from sales of Arthritis 
Foundation products fund research to 
find a cure for arthritis. 

Aspirin is one segment of the anal- 


gesics category that has seen its sales 
decline as other pain relievers gained 
favor. However, its fortunes could be 
in for a reversal during 1995 consider- 
ing developments surrounding the 
Bayer brand of aspirin. Its fortunes 
this past year have been hard to keep 
track of since Eastman Kodak sold 
Bayer manufacturer’ Sterling 
Winthrop to SmithKline Beecham. 
SmithKline then sold Sterling’s U.S. 
consumer health operations to the 
German pharmaceutical company 
Bayer AG. Bayer’s U.S. pharmaceuti- 
cal subsidiary, Miles, will be com- 
bined with Sterling to form a new con- 
sumer care division that will market 
Bayer aspirin. Bayer AG, which first 
developed aspirin in 1897, lost its 
U.S. trademark rights when German 
property was seized as part of World 
War I reparations. Marketing plans to 
rebuild Bayer and perhaps renew in- 


terest in aspirin have yet to be dis- 
closed

Contraceptives worth the cost | 


by Walter Alexander 


aying for higher-priced contra- 
ception saves more than paying 
for less expensive contraception, 


according to an American Academy of 


Family Physicians (AAFP) 46th An- 
nual Scientific Assembly presenta- 
tion. Furthermore, an economic model 
| (see chart) comparing direct medical 
costs of 15 contraceptive methods to 
no contraception found contraception 
to be cost-effective overall 

Despite a broad range of contracep- 
tive options, 3.5 million U.S. women 
become pregnant unintentionally 
every year, according to Felicia H 
Stewart, M.D., Deputy Assistant Sec- 
retary for Population Affairs, Depart- 
ment of Health and Human Services 
Of those becoming pregnant, nearly 
half used some form of contraception 
About half of the pregnancies occu 
ring with contraceptives are due ti 
improper use, and the other half are 
attributable to the inherent fallibility 
of the methods themselves, Dr. Stew- 
art commented. She noted that 40 
million U.S. women age 15 to 44 are 
at risk for unintentional pregnancie 

“The net cost of the ensuing preg- 
nancies to our health resources is 
well over $1 billion per year,” Dr 
Stewart said. Those costs arise from 
approximately 1.6 million therapeu- 


RX REPORT 


tic abortions and from the medical 
care given to another 1.9 million 
women who experience spontaneous 
abortion, ectopic pregnancy or full 
term delivery, Dr. Stewart said 


Three new methods 


Women have three recently ap 
proved contraceptive methods avail 
able to them 
Stewart, is a hormonal drug delivered 
through an implant in the arm (Nor 
plant®), that is highly effective for up 
A second is an injectable 


One, according to Dr 


to 5 years 
hormone (Depo-Provera®) effective for 
up to 3 months. Both are reversible 
and both are more effective and easier 
to use than oral contraceptives, Dr 
Stewart noted. The third recently ap 
proved method, the female condom, is 
less effective at preventing pregnancy, 
she said, although it .«!es prevent 
sexually transmitted diseases 

In terms of the economics of care, 
and in the context of managed care, 
understanding the first two has been 
a problem. “The implant end the in- 


jectables have a fairly high price tag, 


especially up front,” Dr. Stewart 
said. That uncertainty provided the 
incentive for Wyeth-Ayerst, the mak- 
er of Norplant®, to fund Dr. Stew- 
art’s study. She emphasized, howey 
er, that Wyeth-Ayerst had no control 
over the study’s author commitee 
The study revealed that the most 


cost-effective reversible methods were 
the implant and injectable contracep- 
tives, and the most expensive were the 
sponge and cervical cap (see chart). 
High up-front costs, the study showed, 
are offset when an effective contracep- 
tive method reduces the incidence of 


pregnancies, abortions and births. | 
Wider coverage of zontraceptive costs, | 
Dr. Stewart suggested, would lead to | 
broader contraceptive use, fewer unin- | 
tended pregnancies and therefore few- | 
er ectopic pregnancies, spontaneous | 
abortions, births and newborn hospi- 
talizations that are often covered 

“If improving coverage improves 
women’s ability to use the more effec- 
tive methods, the time horizon before 
the investment becomes cost-effective 

within 1 or 2 years—isn’t very 
long,” Dr. Stewart said. Presently, at 
least a third of the women who need 
financial help to pay for contraception 
are not getting it, she added 

For payers and policy analysts, the 
key question is clearly whether an in- 
crease in contraceptive coverage 
would affect the bottom line. “The 
fundamental finding,” Dr. Stewart 
said in an interview for F7P, “is that 
effective methods are the biggest cost

New drugs ‘94: Is the FDA 
killing the Golden Goose? 


by Ken Rankin 


he Food and Drug Administra- 
tion’s (FDA) beefed-up drug ap- 
proval staff set speed records for 
reviewing New Drug Applications 
(NDAs) last year, but fewer new med- 
icines are reaching pharmacy shelves. 

Generic approvals are also down, 
and the number of “new molecular 
entities” (NMEs) that cleared the 
FDA drug approval 
maze in 1994 dropped 
to the lowest level 
since the 1980s. 

Worse yet, most of 
the NMEs that won 
approval from FDA 
last year are drugs 
that have been avail- 
able to pharmacists 
and patients in Eu- 
rope and other coun- 
tries for years. 

Critics of the 
agency say last year’s 
performance in ap- 
proving NDAs is fresh 
evidence that drastic 
reforms are needed to 
address the regulatory 
rigor mortis at FDA. 

As they see it, FDA 
is an overly cautious 
bureaucracy, commit- 
ted to hidebound drug 
approval procedures 
that uanecessarily 
keep important new 
pharmaceuticals off 
the market. Despite 
adding hundreds of 
new drug reviewers 
under the agency’s 
new “user fee” system, 
the FDA’s “zero-risk mentality” con- 
tinues to discourage industry re- 
search and development, and denies 
Americans access to potentially life- 
saving new medicines. 

In response to these concerns, 
FDA “reformers” in Congress and the 
pharmaceutical industry are actively 
considering radical surgery for the 
agency, including “privatizing” key 


FDA functions such as the review of 
new drug applications. 

Much of the dissatisfaction with 
FDA intensified following its perfor- 
mance in 1994. Here’s the bottom line: 

The FDA approved 304 new drugs, 
biologicals and generic products last 
year, down 18 percent from the 370 
cleared for marketing in 1993. 

Only 62 new drugs made it through 
the FDA approval maze in 1994, down 
from 70 the prior year and 91 in 1992. 


Approvals of new biologics plunged 
by 55 percent to 23 products, down 
from 51 the previous year. 

Generic drug approvals also de- 
clined in 1994, falling 12 percent 
from 249 in 1993 to 219 last year. 

Most disturbing, perhaps, was the 
decline in the number of approvals of 
NMEs—drugs that are distinctly dif- 
ferent in structure from those already 


on the market. 

Last year the FDA cleared only 22 
NMEs, down from 25 the year before 
and well below the agency’s pre-user 
fee high-water mark of 30 in 1991. 

For their part, the FDA officials re- 
gard this criticism as a “bum rap.” The 
agency’s drug review staff experienced 
“significantly improved performance” 
last year, FDA officials said. “The 
most important gains were in median 
approval times” for new drugs, which 
dropped from 24 
months in 1993 to 19 
months last year, ac- 
cording to the FDA. 

The average review 
time for biological 
products shortened 
even more dramatical- 
ly in 1994, as did the 
FDA’s reviews of new 
molecular entities. For 
the 22 NMEs ap- 
proved last year, the 
median review time 
was 17.5 months— 
down from 23 months 
in 1993. 

FDA’s goal is to re- 
duce the average new 
drug review time to 12 
months by 1997. Al- 
though that’s still 
twice as long as the 
maximum review time 
required by law, the 
FDA officials said if 
they accomplish their 
objectives, drugs will 
be “approved faster 
here than anywhere 
else in the world.” 

According to the 
FDAers, these short- 
er approval times are 
a direct result of the new “user fee” 
law which imposes charges on NDA 
sponsors to finance a massive $320 
million build-up in the agency’s new 
drug review team. 

All told, the FDA has been autho- 
rized to hire 600 new chemists, phar- 
macologists, biologists and other drug 
review personnel, nearly half of whom 

Continued on page 14 


DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, MARCH 1995 


13 


COVER STORY 


Is the Golden Goose on the line? 


Continued from page 13 
were on board at the agency by the 
end of last year. 

But while the extra staffing ap- 
pears to have helped shorten average 
NDA review times, it hasn’t pro- 
duced any increase in approvals. 

For their part, pharmaceutical in- 
dustry leaders say the declining 
number of NDA approvals is not due 
to any downturn in new product re- 
search and development activities by 
manufacturers. 

Officials at the Pharmaceutical Re- 
| search and Manufacturers of America 
(PhRMA) note that, if anything, in- 
dustry is adding more new drug can- 
didates to the pipeline—an observa- 
tion that FDA officials confirm. 

In the agency’s latest report to 
Congress on its implementation of 
the user fee program, FDA officials 
revealed that the number of industry 
submissions for new drugs and bio- 
logics “is growing at a rate of more 
than 20 percent per year.” 


Who’s to blame? 


But if more drugs are entering the 
pipeline and the FDA reviewers are 
clearing them faster, where’s the bot- 
tleneck? According to PhRMA presi- 
dent Gerald Mossinghoff, the prob- 
lem is in the requirements imposed 
on new drug sponsors prior to the 
filing of an NDA. 

On average, it takes a decade of 
extensive clinical and “pre-clinical” 
testing before an NDA can even be 
submitted to the FDA for review. 

These costly studies—which often 
must duplicate research conducted in 
other countries—are the primary 
reason that the cost of developing the 
average new drug has mushroomed 
to more than $350 million. 

But aside from the cost of these 
studies, current FDA testing require- 
ments are the primary reason that 
Americans are often among the last to 
benefit from the development of new 
medicines. 

Of the 22 NMEs approved by the 
FDA last year, at least 14 had already 
been on the market in other countries 
from Belgium to Bulgaria, often for 
years. 

One of the most important new 
drugs cleared for U.S. marketing in 
1994—Lipha Pharmaceuticals’ Glu- 
cophage (metformin HCl) oral hyper- 


é ae. 


FDA is approving new drugs faster... 
...but fewer NMEs are reaching the market

Source: U.S. Food and Drug Administration 


glycemia treatment—had been avail- 
able to patients with diabetes in 
France, Britain and 90 other coun- 
tries for more than 20 years. 

If anything, pharmaceutical indus- 
try leaders fear that the “U.S. drug 
lag” could widen if the FDA drug de- 
velopment and approval process is 
not reformed. 

Of particular concern is the contin- 
ued ability of U.S. firms to compete 
in the booming biotech drug market. 
Although FDA’s Center for Biologics 
Evaluation and Research now boasts 
a staff of 776 scientists, reviewers 
and support personnel, the agency 
managed to approve only one new 
biotechnology medicine last year. 


Biologics leadership threatened 


American firms have traditionally 
dominated the development of these 
drugs, but biotech pioneer George 
Rathmann contends that U.S. leader- 
ship in this area “is now seriously 
threatened” by the FDA’s over-cau- 
tious approval policies. 

Rathmann, who founded Amgen 


and now serves as chief executive of- 
ficer at ICOS Corp., contends that 
“American leadership in biotechnolo- 
gy cannot be sustained in the face of 
the FDA’s ‘zero-risk’ requirements 
for the approval of new biologics.” 

A key part of the problem, he said, 
is “the excessive caution of the FDA 
regulators during early human clini- 
cal trials of new medicines.” 

After recommending a “reasonable 
risk level” for these studies, new 
drug sponsors are typically urged by 
FDA officials to decrease dosage lev- 
els, establish lengthy observation pe- 
riods and schedule discussions with 
the agency at each individual state of 
the trials, he said. 

“The reviewers’ explanation often 
is only that they are ‘more comfort- 
able with a more cautious approach,” 
Rathmann said. “It’s to the point 
that you can’t get out of Phase I tri- 
als in the United States.” 

Other FDA critics, such as the 
Washington Legal Foundation, said 
that patients are likely to be the real 
victims of this kind of “zero risk’ 


14 


DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, MARCH 1995 i 


mentality. 

“If a murderer kills you, it’s homi- 
cide,” the group said in a recent 
newspaper ad. “If the FDA kills you, 
it’s just being cautious.” 

This kind of anti-FDA sentiment is 
not limited to the private sector. The 
new GOP Congress has expressed 
sharp criticism of the agency and is 
expected to put FDA Commissioner 
David Kessler and his drug review 
staff on the hot seat this year. 


Senate Labor and Human Re- 
sources Committee chair Nancy 
Kassebaum (R-Kan.) has already an- 
nounced plans for a series of over- 
sight hearings examining the FDA’s 
policies and procedures— including 
the agency’s questionable practice of 
rushing through approvals of impor- 
tant new drugs at the end of the year 
to pad NME totals. 

In 1994, over 40 percent of the 
NMEs approved during the year were 


‘New Molecular Entities” approved in 1994 


cleared for marketing during Decem- 
ber, but critics of the agency suspect 
Kassebaum may be one of the friend- 
liest faces Kessler sees on Capitol Hill 
this year. Rep. Thomas Bliley (R-Va.), 
chairman of the House Commerce 
Committee, which oversees the FDA, 
is predicting flatly that this year Con- 
gress will enact “reforms in the way 
that the Food and Drug Administra- 
tion does business.”

Continued from page 15 

Like other critics of the agency, 
Bliley contends that the FDA unrea- 
sonably withholds approvals for new 
medicines. “It just breaks my heart 
when I think of American citizens hav- 
ing to go to Switzerland or Mexico to 
get the drugs and devices they need to 
stay alive because the Washington bu- 
reaucracy won’t approve them,” he 


said last month in a speech to the Na- 
tional Committee for Quality Health 
Care. 

House Speaker Newt Gingrich is 
even more hostile. Proposing a stem- 
to-stern restructuring of the agency, 
Gingrich denounced the FDA as “the 
leading job killer in America” and de- 
rided Kessler as “a thug and a bully.” 

One conservative organization
