* Towne-Oller survey — July 1994. 


that has been particularly critical of 
the FDA—the Competitive Enter- 
prise Institute—has even proposed 
allowing the marketing of unap- 
proved drugs and medical devices, as 
long as they carry warning labels 
alerting the public to their status. 

Another plan gaining support on 
Capitol Hill would require the FDA 
to continue assuring the safety of 
new drugs, but backpeddle from cur- 
rent requirements that they be 
proven effective as well. 

Not all of the criticism of the 
FDA’s drug approval practices is 
coming from the political right, how- 
ever. Even Gingrich’s attacks on the 
agency are mild in comparison to re- 
cent criticism of the FDA by militant 
AIDS activists. Although the FDA re- 
sponded by granting top priority to 
approving new HIV drugs, that poli- 
cy has already produced a backlash 
from other groups concerned that re- 
views of non-AIDS drugs are being 
delayed as a result. 


Not everyone’s a critic 


The FDA does have some defenders 
in Washington, including officials at 
Public Citizen’s Health Research 
Group (HRG) who argue that speeding 
the agency’s new drug reviews could 
endanger the safety of U.S. medicines. 

A freshly released HRG study of 
pharmaceutical withdrawals in the 
U.S. and Europe concludes that the 
FDA’s current regulatory process 
“protects people from a large number 
of products that kill and injure peo- 
ple in other countries.” 

Between 1970 and 1992, the study 
found that 31 drugs had to be re- 
moved from the market in France, 20 
were withdrawn in the United King- 
dom and 23 were removed in Britain. 
In the U.S., however, HRG reports 
that only 9 drugs had to be with- 
drawn for safety reasons by the FDA. 

“By having lower safety and effica- 
cy standards, other countries are 
making guinea pigs out of their pop- 
ulation,” an HRG official said. 

Critics of the FDA don’t dispute 
HRG’s statistics, but do take exception 
to the group’s logic. The 56 drugs cov- 
ered by HRG’s study were responsible 
for several dozen reported deaths in 
the countries where they had been ap- 
proved, they noted. the FDA’s delays 
in approving other drugs for use in the 
U.S. have been responsible for thou- 
sands of unnecessary deaths in this 
country, they charged. FTP

nailing major contracts to 
provide pharmacy ser-

had landed only 13 small 
contracts—thne largest 
with Fuji covers only 
about 2,000 “lives.”

NACDS president Ron 
Ziegler, offered no expla- 
nation for the changes but 
expressed confidence in 
“the tremendous potential 
of PDN.” Until a new chief 
executive officer is named 
the network will be man- 
aged by an operating com 
mittee of PDN’s board with 
§ | help from chain drug indus- 
try veteran Don Hoscheit

NEWS & TRENDS 


Pharmacy tech Between 4,000 and 6,090 "certified" pharmacy technician 
wannabes are expected to kick in $95 this year to take the two- 


- -~ 7” 

certification goal: and-a-half-hour exam under development by the new Pharmacy 
Technician Certification Board (PTCB). The board, which was 

4,000 to 6,000 created by the American Pharmaceutical Association, the Ameri- 
can Society of Health-System Pharmacists, ane Illinois and 
Michigan Pharmacists Associations, will offer the first of three 
1995 exams on July 29 in locations across the country. Mean- 
while two groups opposed to pharmacy technician certification 
(PTC)—NARD and NACDS—-began distributing their own tech- 
nician training manual last week. 


FoxMeyer FoxMeyer Health Corp.’s information management services sub- 
mae sidiary, HealthConnect, announced its intention to acquire as- 
subsidiary to sets of Pharmaceutical Care Services, Inc. of Waco, Texas, for 
- undisclosed terms. Included in those assets is the PharmCare 
acquire PharmCare program, one of the leading pharmaceutical care models for en- 
hancing the practice of pharmacy. The PharmCare program, 
which will operate within HealthConnect, has been introduced 
in around 200 pharmacies nationwide.

Dueling videos The lobbying war over state legislation to curb discriminatory 
pan pricing by drug makers took on a high tech tilt as both sides re- 
heat up equal leased new videos to support their cause. A NACDS/NARD tape 
an tells legislators across the country that pharmaceutical manu- 
access battle facturers charge sharply higher prices to community pharma- 
cists, driving up prescription costs to retail customers. 
The Pharmaceutical Research and Manufacturers of America’s 
(PhRMA) response video doesn't refute that accusation, but it 
does claim that NACDS and NARD are “wrong and misleading” 
to suggest that drug makers “arbitrarily” offer a special class of 
trade discounts to institutions. PhRMA's video contains some 
embarrassing factual errors though, such as citing a 17 percent 
to 24 percent after-tax net profit for retailers on prescriptions 
not the 2 percent to 3 percent cited in the NACDS/NARD video. 


APhA backs 


RS American Pharmaceutical Association's (APhA) executive vice pres- 

petition to delay ident John Gans urged Food and Drug Administration (FDA) offi- 
cials to “err on the side of caution” and reconsider plans to approve 

estrogen AN DAs ANDAs for generic versions of Wyeth-Ayerst's Premarin® conjugat- 
ed estrogen product. At issue: the manufacturer's claim that generic 
versions are not “pharmaceutically equivalent” unless they contain 
all six of the innovator drug's estrogen components. Since third-par- 
ty plans would likely mandate substitution for generic Premarin if 
it is approved, Gans said that "America's pharmacists do not want 
the FDA to put them in a position where they can only be paid for 
dispensing a product in which they have little confidence."

DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, APRIL 1995 


OTC NEWS 


First aid sales are healthy — Diabetes patients | 


Shorter hospitals stays, increased 
patient self-care and new product in- 
troductions propelled sales of first aid 
items to record heights during 1994, 
according to sales figures compiled by 
Information Resources, Inc., a Chica- 
go-based market research company. 
The category of first aid accessories, 
including items such as bandages, 
muscle and body support devices, dia- 
betes test kits, thermometers, heat 
and ice packs and first aid kits, grew 
to $721 million during 1994 and out- 
paced all other OTC categories with a 
10 percent sales increase from the 
previous year. 

First aid accessories continues to be 
a core category for both chain and in- 
dependent drug stores. Drug’s market 
share is roughly 62 percent, compared 
with 16 percent for food stores and 22 
percent for mass merchandisers. Only 
the OTC categories of internal anal- 
gesics, vitamins and cough/cold 
recorded higher drug store sales than 
first aid. 

The category of first aid treat- 


Drug stores dominate 
first aid sales 


Source: Information Resource 
Based on sales for 52 week 
ments, defined by IRI insect-re- 


lated first aid products, smelling 
salts, anti-itch products and oint- 
ments, was also a strong category for 
drug stores during 1994. Sales in- 
creased more than three percent to 
$173 million giving drug more than 
a 51 percent market share 


must test more 


Further evidence that diabetes 
patients provide an excellent coun- 
seling opportunity for pharmacists 
comes from a recent study conduct- 
ed by LifeScan Inc. The manufac- 
turer of the market leading One 
Touch brand blood glucose monitor 
and strips commissioned the 
Gallup Organization to survey 276 
primary care physicians. The key 
finding was that half of the nation’s 
seven million people diagnosed 
with Type I (insulin dependent) di- 
abetes don’t follow their doctors’ ad- 
vice to manage their disease 
through blood glucose monitoring. 
Despite doctor encouragement, only 
56 percent of the Type I patients 
monitor their blood glucose levels 
with an average testing frequency 
of 1.2 times per day. That is well 
below the American Diabetes Asso- 
ciation recommended guideline of 
three to four times per day. 


Phytochemicals: next nutritionals trend? 


Sales of vitamins and herbs sky- 
rocketed in recent years as consumers 
were bombarded with extensive media 
reports about the benefits of nutrition- 
al supplementation. Now, judging 
from media reports, indications are 
the next hot trend in the category will 
be phytochemicals. Several of the ma- 
jor nutritional product manufacturers 
have introduced such products, or will 
be shortly. Phytochemicals, which are 
derived from plants, primarily fruits 
and vegetables, are praised for their 
cancer fighting abilities. Product mar- 
keters believe phytochemical products 
have strong consumer appeal since an 
estimated nine out of 10 Americans 


don't eat the Food and Drug Adminis- 
tration’s recommended five daily serv- 
ings of fruits and vegetables. 

Leading vitamin manufacturer 
Leiner Health Products has intro- 
duced three products under the 


Your Life Phyto-Nutrient brand 
name: broccoli, mixed vegetable and 


spinach. “Your Life Phyto-Nutrients 


are not intended to substitute for a 
balanced, healthy diet,” said Leiner 
president Gale Bensussen. “They 


are designed for people who find it 
difficult to eat a healthy diet.” 

Other major manufacturers sell 
phytochemical lines through health 
food stores, but those product lines 


and other new lines will likely debut 
soon in mass market outlets, accord- 
ing to the marketing director of an- 
other major vitamin manufacturer 
The introduction of phytochemicals 
comes as nutritional product manu- 
facturers are enjoying new found 
freedom to make claims about the 
beneficial properties of their prod- 
ucts following passage of the Dietary 
Supplement Health and Education 
Act of 1994. As a result, pharmacists 
can expect to see new claims appear- 
ing on product labels and the ap- 
pearance of manufacturer sponsored 
educational displays and literature 
In stores. 


OTC labeling opposition intensifies 


Manufacturers of over-the-counter 
drugs are readying themselves to de- 
fend FDA attacks on OTC labels and 
state legislative efforts that could 
hinder the manufacture and distrib- 
ution of OTCs. Already in 1995, the 
Nonprescription Drug Manufactur- 
ers Association (NDMA) is working 
on defeating or amending 22 state 
bills the trade association contends 
would break the nation’s uniform 
OTC regulatory system. During 
1994, 43 such bills were defeated or 


26 


amended, NDMA president Jim 
Cope said recently at the group’s an- 
nual meeting 

The biggest challenge for the 
trade group and manufacturers will 
later this year when the FDA 
firms up plans to redo OTC labels 
FDA can expect strong resistance 
from manufacturers if it goes ahead 
with a plan requiring additional ac- 
tive ingredient information to be 
listed on the front label of the prod- 
uct, also known as the —— dis- 


come 


play panel (PDP). “We draw the line 
at cluttering the PDP,” said Bob 
Donovan, a senior vice president at 
Sandoz and NDMA chairman. Head- 
ing a special task force to work with 
the FDA on ensuring the product la- 
bel as a marketing tool is Tom 
Moore, president of Procter & Gam- 
ble Healthcare Products 

The FDA’s effort is intended to pro- 
duce a more “user friendly” OTC label 
that will be easier for consumers to 
read and comprehend.

Barton promises A new Congressional investigation into retail pharmacy’s 


charges of discriminatory pricing by drug manufacturers is in 
equal access the works. Rep. Joe Barton (R-Texas), a former GOP back- 
bencher on Rep. John Dingell’s House investigations subcommit- 
probe this year | tee, proposed such a probe last year, but no action was taken. 
Now that Barton chairs Dingell’s old subcommittee, the equal 
access investigation will get underway, he told NARD’s National 
Legislation conference. (See p. 50 for Barton’s other plan to 
skewer the Food and Drug Administration’s “sacred cows.”) 


New California | The California Pharmacists Association’s efforts to establish 


o new guidelines on pharmacist working conditions—complete 
workload guides | with a “model” employee contract for bench pharmacists — is stir- 
d fi ring objections from drug chains throughout the state. The 

raw rire | CPhA’s “guidelines for optimal professional relationships” would 
limit “routine” pharmacist work hours to 10 per day (90 hours in 
any two-week period), require rest breaks every two to three 
| hours, cap pharmacist dispensing at 15 prescriptions per hour 
and prohibit supervision of pharmacists by non-professionals. 
| Chain execs say the plan smacks of labor unionism.

of F awel i managed ton,” NARD president Gene Graves warned independents at the as- 

sociation’s legislative conference. Graves’ concern: a new “ERISA re- 
care bill... form bill” pushed by Rep. Harris Fawell that would repeal all 
“anti-managed care” laws, including those guaranteeing consumers 
freedom of choice of pharmacy. “Let’s sink this part of Fawell’s bill 
while it’s still in the harbor,” Graves told the NARD faithful.

$4 cow drugmakers Pharmaceutical industry “vertical integration” into PBMs and 


| similar “alliances” between drug manufacturers and managed 

hope to save care providers can reduce U.S. health care costs by as much as 

sean $200 billion annually, a new Warner-Lambert sponsored study 

x 200 billion claims. At the same time, the report predicts that under such an 

“integrated” system, spending for prescription drugs would 

mushroom from 8 percent of total health costs to 15 percent and 

that pharmacists would become more deeply involved in “treat- 
ment protocols and monitoring outcomes.”

DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, MAY 1995 


Retail pharmacy 1995: moving 
ahead on an alternate path 


by Ken Rankin 


year ago pharmacists were rid- 
ing high on the road to health 
| care reform as the Clinton Ad- 


| ministration drove hard for legisla- 
tion that addressed many of commu- 
| nity pharmacy’s deepest concerns. 
But when the President’s health re- 
form bandwagon hit a roadblock in 
Congress last fall, pharmacy leaders 
| were forced to shift gears and map 
out a new route to achieve their goals. 

Today pharmacy is moving ahead 
on an alternate path—a more diffi- 
cult road with unexpected twists, 
steep hills and dangerous potholes 
along the way. But while the course 
| is different, the objectives of retail 
| pharmacy leaders remain the same. 

Top priority continues to be gaining 
| “equal access” to the price discounts 
| that many pharmaceutical manufac- 
turers offer selectively to HMOs, 
mail-order dispensaries and other 
| managed care pharmacy providers. 
| And rank and file retail pharmacy 
| practitioners appear in full agree- 
| ment that NARD and the National 
| Association of Chain Drug Stores 
(NACDS) are on the right track in 
| pressing hard for an end to “discrimi- 
| natory” pricing by drug makers. 

A new nationwide survey sched- 
uled to be published in next month’s 
Drug Store News For the Pharmacist 
found that today nearly nine out of 
10 community pharmacists believe 
that “much of what ails retail phar- 
macy practice today could be cured if 
drug manufacturers were required to 
offer community pharmacists the 
same prices that are available to 
mail order, institutional and man- 
aged care providers.” 

But reform of pharmaceutical in- 
dustry pricing practices is only one of 
the issues on retail pharmacy’s 
crowded agenda. Drug chains and 
independents are also wrestling 
with a series of other con- 
cerns ranging from 
the ethical im- 


plications of pharmacist incentive 
“switch” programs, to the debate over 
the ramifications of voluntary certifi- 
cation of pharmacy technicians. 
Here’s where things stand now on 
some of the most pressing issues fac- 
ing community pharmacy: 
Discriminatory pricing: With the 
demise of President Clinton’s health 
care reform plan, the battleground 
over “equal access” to drug manufac- 
turer discounts shifted from Washing- 
ton to the states. NARD and NACDS 
are pushing a new “model” bill to out- 
law discriminatory pricing practices 
by pharmaceutical makers that is ex- 
pected to be introduced in 30 legisla- 
tures across the country this session. 
The measure has drawn heavy opposi- 
tion from pharmaceutical industry lob- 
byists who have managed to defeat 
the proposal in South 
Dakota and several A 
other states. 


Regardless of what happens on the 
legislative front, a series of class ac- | 
tion lawsuits filed by drug chains, in- 
dependents and supermarket pharma- 
cy operators are threatening to force 
pharmaceutical manufacturers to 
abandon their controversial “class of 
trade” discount structure as a viola- 
tion of the federal antitrust laws. 

Meanwhile, regulatory officials in 
two states where drug price discrimi- 
nation is prohibited are actively in- 
vestigating suspected illegal practices 
by pharmaceutical manufacturers, In 
Maine, the state attorney general is 
probing a possible conspiracy by ma- 
jor drug makers to raise prices last 
summer in response to that state’s 
new requirement that retail pharma- 
cies be offered the same discounts 
available to HMOs and nursing home 
pharmacies. 

In Wisconsin, where pharmaceuti- 
cal price discrimination has been ille- 
gal for years, regulators are question- 
ing retail and institutional pharmacies 
to determine whether drug manufac- 
turers are violating the law. 

Third-party reimbursement: 
OBRA ’90’s moratorium on state reim- 
bursement cuts to participating Med- 
icaid pharmacies expired on Jan. 1, 
1995—a development that triggered 
proposals in a number of states to 
slash dispensing fees to community 

pharmacists. At the same time, a 

growing number of states are 
seeking waivers to shift Med- 
icaid patients to “managed 
care” providers—a move 
that could lock many 
retail pharmacies out 
of the program. 
There’s even

Staying on top of the game 


by Nancy Maline 


4 Ns game never seems as simple 
as it used to be. It’s getting 
even more difficult to distin- 

guish teammates from opponents. 


There are new players in the form of 
chains growing larger through acqui- 


sitions; new coaches are the man- 
aged care organizations; and the op- 
posing teams are wearing mass mer- 
chandiser, supermarket and mail 
order uniforms. All the while, the 
general manager is continuously 
changing government legislation.

the Pharmacist’s Annual Survey

“Chain drug stores are defined as part of a group of 4 or more units 
Source: Drug Store News For the Pharmacist; /MS. 


Pharmacy volume through chains has increased 300 percent since 
1985; independent pharmacy sales are up 150 percent. 


show that, even with the industry 
facing once in a lifetime changes, a 
new and stronger drug industry is 
emerging—one that has learned 
what it takes to stay in the game. 


Class of trade wars 


It is indeed a high stakes game, 
with sales of pharmacy products 
reaching $62 billion in 1994. Chain 
drug stores hold a 37 percent share 
of total retail pharmacy sales, at 
$23.2 billion. Independent drug 
stores’ share is 29 percent of sales, or 
$18.3 billion. Pharmacy sales by 
mail were $7.1 billion in 1994, fol- 
lowed by mass merchants at $7 bil- 
lion, and food stores at $6.6 billion. 

Drug Store News For the Pharma- 
cist conducted an exclusive survey of 
the major players in pharmacy. Our 
roster shows that the top retailers 
are not always drug stores. Howev- 
er, not all discounters do the volume 
of Wal-Mart or Kmart, and not all 
supermarkets have pharmacy sales 
as high as Kroger, Albertson’s or 
Safeway. Although mass merchants 
and supermarkets are not focused on 
pharmacy the way drug stores are, 
those retailers in the visitor’s dugout 
are scoring big, and on turf that once 
belonged exclusively to traditional 
drug stores. 


Independent stores vs. chains 


It is not by accident that drug 
stores outperform all other classes of 
trade 2-to-1 in sales of pharmaceuti- 
cals. Larger drug chains have gained 
strength in numbers, expanding by 
adding new stores and through major 
acquisitions. The smaller chains are 
thinking more like large chains. In 
1994 there were 241 stores added to 
the chain drug mix for a total of 
17,270 stores. 

While the number of independents 
has decreased (from 26,267 in 1993 
to 24,862), sales have held steady. 
Independent drug stores filled $18.3 
billion worth of prescriptions, repre- 
senting 29 percent of the retail phar- 
macy business. Although the in- 
crease in drug sales was less than 
two percent from 1993 to 1994, there 
are now 1,400 fewer stores generat- 
ing that volume. 

Our annual survey results indicate 
that, despite the obvious differences 
between independents and chains, 


Continued on page 33 


DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, MAY 1995 


COVER STORY 


Staying on top of the game 


Continued from page 30 
both formats are drug stores 
with basic similarities. For ex- 
ample, average prescription 
prices are almost identical, re- 
gardless of whether the script is 
filled at a chain or an indepen- 
dent store. Because more of the 
prescriptions they fill are 
branded and therefore more 
costly, chain stores charge 
about 20 cents more per script 
on average ($27.40 vs. $27.18 
for independents). Indepen- 
dents use a higher markup, 
however. The average gross 
margin for a prescription filled 
at an independent is 27.7 per- 
cent; 24.3 percent for a chain 
store. 

It appears that the prescrip- 
tion business is in a growth 
mode in both chains and inde- 
pendents since new prescrip- 
tions outpace refills (56 percent 
new for independents vs. 52 
percent for chains). Perhaps 
reflecting a greater concern for 
the bottom line, independent 
drug stores are 5 percent more 
likely than chains to dispense 
generic drugs (48 percent for 
independents, 43 percent for 
chains). Since chains do a 
higher volume of business, the 
dollar value of their inventory 
at any given time is higher, av- 
eraging $101,693 at cost, com- 
pared to $66,522 per store for 
independents. In addition, a 


chain drug store reporis inven- 
tory turns an average of 10 
times a year, where an inde- 
pendent averages eight turns. 

As for special services pro- 
vided by the drug stores, com- 
munity pharmacies are more 
likely to “go the extra mile” for 
their customers. Over two- 
thirds of independents (68 per- 
cent) will deliver a prescrip- 
tion, vs. only 28 percent of 
chain drug companies. 

The OBRA regulations ap- 
pear to have had a greater ef- 
fect on independent pharma- 
cies, at least to this point. Six 
out of every 10 independents 
(60 percent) provide a private 
area for counseling, compared 
to 34 percent of drug chain 
companies. Regardless of ori- 
entation, all pharmacies tend 
to employ an average of two 
technicians per store. Chain 
stores are as likely as indepen- 
dents to have drive-through 
windows in at least some of 
their stores (8 percent each). 

The early 1990s were defined 
by huge increases in the cost of 
an average prescription. Dur- 
ing those years, it was not un- 
usual to see a change of be- 
tween 10 percent and 20 
percent per year. In the past 
five years, average prescription 
prices have gone from $17 in 
1989, to over $27 in 1994, rep-

There were price changes for nearly three out of every 10 prescription 
drugs last year. Interestingly, after declining an average of 4 percent the 
previous year, generic drug prices increased at twice the rate of brands. 


Clerks were busy last year placing 
new price stickers on drug store 
products. According to Medi-Span 
(Indianapolis, Ind.), there were 
18,608 prescription and OTC prod- 
ucts whose prices either increased or 
decreased during 1994. Nearly a 
third of those prescription products 
whose prices changed were from the 
top 200 list. Branded and generic 
products had approximately the 
same number of price changes. How- 
ever the price of a generic drug took 
a huge jump, increasing 8.4 percent 
in 1994, compared to a 4 percent de- 
crease the year before. 

The primary provider of prescrip- 
tion products continues to be the 
chain drug store, dispensing 25 per- 
cent of the 2 billion plus prescrip- 
tions filled in 1994. Hospital dispen- 
saries filled 22 percent of all 
prescriptions. Independent drug 
stores garnered a 19 percent share of 
all scripts filled, down from nearly 21 
percent in 1993. The gainers in the 
prescription business were clinics 
(4.7 percent vs. 3.5 percent in 1993), 
mass merchants (7.5 percent, up 
from 7.1 percent) and nursing homes 
(3.7 percent vs. 3.4 percent). FTP

Vertical integration: Discriminatory pric- 
ing isn’t the only issue causing friction between 
pharmacists and drug manufacturers. Merck’s 
1993 takeover of Medco and similar moves last 
year by SmithKline Beecham (Diversified 
Pharmaceutical Services) and Lilly (PCS) have 
raised new competitive concerns among phar- 
macists. Although government antitrusters al- 
lowed all three mergers to take effect, the Fed- 
eral Trade Commission is now reinvestigating 
the anticompetitive implications of these verti- 
cal integration moves. The U.S. General Ac- 
counting Office has also been asked to examine 
the acquisition of PBMs by pharmaceutical 
manufacturers, and lawyers for the Pharmacy 
Defense Fund recently secured a court injunc- 
tion requiring Merck/Medco to offer an open 
formulary to its third-party customers. 

Retail pharmacy, meanwhile, launched an 
ambitious initiative of its own to compete head 
on in the third-party market by forming its 
own PBM. The new “Pharmacy Direct Net- 
work” (established with seed money from 
NACDS board members but operated indepen- 
dently from that organization) quickly attract- 
ed members operating some 34,000 chain and 
independent pharmacies nationwide. 

Securing third-party contracts to provide 
pharmacy services through the new network 
proved more difficult, however. After nine 
months the company had nailed down only a 
handful of contracts, PDN president Robert 
Pfotenhauer and other key staffers were 
abruptly terminated, and the future of the or- 
ganization appeared to be in question. 

Pharmaceutical care: Community phar- 
macists launched new efforts last year to trans- 
late the principles of pharmaceutical care into 
use in a retail setting, but the sticking point re- 
mains securing compensation for cognitive ser- 
vices. One hopeful sign is the growing accep- 
tance of NARD’s new “Pharmaceutical Care 
Claim Form”—a document that enables phar- 
macists to identify patient interventions and 
bill third-party payors for them. Over the past 
year more than 250,000 of these forms have 
been distributed by NARD, and pharmacists 
using them are beginning to receive reimburse- 

Continued on next page 


The May 1994 patent expiration of Tagamet 
did not adversely affect category sales. 
Pepcid and Axid rose 19 percent and 32 
percent respectively, and Zantac was the 
most popular prescription drug in 1994 in 
terms of dollar sales. Prilosec enjoyed a 56 
percent sales boom. The impending OTC 
switch of Tagamet should shift the remain- 
der of its declining sales out front.

The field of pharmacy retailers is becoming more crowded, but it's not only drug stores in the forefront. Mass 
merchants Wal-Mart and Kmart each do over $1 billion in prescription business, and supermarket companies 
like Kroger, Albertson's and Safeway have pharmacy sales that rank them in the top 20 prescription retailers.

There’s less encouraging news 
from Washington state where re- 
searchers have encountered delays in 
the completion of a federally-funded 
study designed to explore options for 
compensating pharmacists for pa- 
tient counseling and other non-dis- 


tributive functions. The findings of 
that demonstration project, which 
had been scheduled for completion at 
the beginning of this year, have been 
delayed until October. 

Underscoring the need for reim- 
bursement for these services is the 
difficulty that many pharmacists 
continue to experience in complying

with the OBRA ‘90 patient counsel-

COVER STORY 


Pharmacist role pivotal in OTC arena 


T's pharmacists’ role in the 
OTC marketplace during 1994 
took on even greater impor- 
tance. New and reformulated OTC 
products and one of the biggest Rx- 
to-OTC switches of the past decade 
(naproxen) came to market as more 
consumers sought self medication. 

Against this backdrop, pharma- 
cists were busier than ever recom- 
mending products and counseling pa- 
tients on their proper use. As a 
result, drug stores maintained a 
dominant market share in 17 key 
OTC categories (see chart) which In- 
formation Resources Inc. tracked for 
Drug Store News for the Pharmacist. 
Categories such as vitamins, first 
aid, antacids, incontinence and foot 
care were drug’s strongest sales gain- 
ers. Other categories such as 
cough/cold, analgesics, eye/lens care 
and contraceptives disappointed with 
sales declines. 

Those declines and market share 


losses, primarily at the hands of 


mass merchandisers, must be taken 
in context, according to chain drug 
retailers and manufacturers. While 
many individual chain drug opera- 
tors reported heaithy gains out front, 
they attribute overali OTC market 
share losses from the drug store cate- 
gory to fewer independent drug 
stores and store closings and finan- 
cial woes at deep discount drug re- 
tailers such as Phar Mor, Drug Em- 
porium and F&M. 


Analgesic addition 


Although the analgesics category 
showed a decline in drug stores, it 
was home to clearly the biggest OTC 
development of 1994 and a new coun- 
seling challenge for pharmacists. The 
midyear introduction of Aleve, Proc- 


ter & Gamble’s OTC version of 


naproxen sodium, quickly captured 
nearly 5 percent of the roughly $1 
billion drug store internal analgesic 
market. Its growth came at the ex- 
pense of ibuprofen, acetaminophen 
and aspirin-containing products. 
Market shares of those active ingre- 
dient segments declined during 1994. 


Regulatory activity 


In addition to product category 
sales trends, the OTC marketplace 
felt the influence of the Food and 
Drug Administration (FDA) and Con-

gress. The passage of the Dietary 
Supplement Health and Education 
Act of 1994 drew praise from manu- 
facturers for the increased latitude it 
gives them to provide consumers 
with information on the purpose and 
significance of dietary supplements. 
The new law also allows literature 
explaining products to be displayed 
in stores. Sales of vitamin and herbal 
products have skyrocketed in recent 
years largely as a result of favorable 
media coverage. The ability of manu- 
facturers to more openly explain 
product benefits is expected to drive 
sales even higher during 1995. 

On another regulatory front, 
FDA’s Nonprescription Drugs Advi- 
sory Committee was also very active 
during 1994, meeting frequently to 
consider Rx-to-OTC switches such as 
SmithKline Beecham’s Tagamet, 
Johnson & Johnson/Merck’s Pepcid, 
Upjohn’s Rogaine and Burroughs 
Wellcome’s Zovirax. While the com- 
mittee voiced concerns about OTC 
availability for all of the products, it 
eventually gave the green light to 


Tagamet earlier this year. At press 
time, SmithKline Beecham was 
awaiting a final approval letter from 
FDA and pharmacists will likely see 
the product later this year. 


Relabeling revisited 


The push for the Rx-to-OTC switch 
of stronger drugs with greater poten- 
tial for interactions layered on a soci- 
ety that enjoys the option of self- 
medicating put FDA’s OTC 
relabeling initiative on the front 
burner last year. The effort to make 
labels more consumer friendly via 
the use of simpler terms, a reorder- 
ing of information and pictograms, 
got rolling during 1994 and is expect- 
ed to really pick up steam during 
1995. Pharmacists may not see the 
new and improved labels during 
1995, but the groundwork has been 
laid that will affect virtually every 
OTC product. A massive reworking 
of OTC labels has the potential to in- 
crease demands on pharmacists as 
consumers familiarize themselves 
with new label formats.

NEWS & TRENDS  [aaneienseeaeTaay 
Food and Drug Administration (FDA) commissioner David 
Kessler has agreed not to mandate pharmacist distribution of 
prescription patient package inserts (PPIs) under his 
MedGuide’” initiative —at least not for five years. But he told 
the National Council on Patient Information and Education 
that if pharmacists don't “voluntarily” provide written pre 
scription information to at least 75 percent of all patients by 
he year 2000, PPI distribution should then become mandato 
ry. Kessler’s problem: with GOP critics of the agency running 
Congress, there may not be an FDA by 2000 


Wyeth-Averst’s efforts to block FDA approval of generic Pre- 
marin represent an attempt to use “the agency's administrative 
process solely to preserve a monopoly,” the Generic Pharmaceu 
tical Industry Association charged. The manufacturer's argu- 
ments against generic conjugated estrogen tablets (which have 
been echoed by APhA) lack “appropriate scientific support” be- 
cause “Wyeth-Ayerst never compared their drug to the generic 
alternative in critical clinical studies on women patients,” 


told FDA 
SATIN HO ER EME IE TSE TN 


New federal! legislation establishing a “transitional class” of 
Rx to OTC drugs available only from pharmacists and pre 
scribers was endorsed by delegates to last month’s National 
Association of Boards of Pharmac \ annual meeting 
NABPers also approved resolutions to replace manufacturer 


drug sampling with a “voucher” system, oppose OTC status 
| 


for carisoprodol, endorse Rep. Bill Brewster's ” 
Compounding Preservation Act” and discourage the use of 
homeopathic products which are not declared safe and effec 


he Food and Drug Administration

(hain and independent pharmacy leaders voiced strong sup- 
port for a separate NABP 
on and “site-specific” training for pharmacy technicians. 
NACDS and NARD, which jointly published their own tech 
training manual recently, called the NABP recommendations 
far superior to “a more onerous, costly certification program” 
being developed by APhA and hospital pharmacy leaders 


resolution endorsing state registra- 


NARD has launched new “National Institute for Pharmacist 
Care Outcomes” to promote “high quality disease management 
and wellness programs” through community pharmacies. Prior 
ities for the new institute include promoting NARD’s existing 
certification programs in respiratory care, diabetes and health 
supports and appliances and expanding acceptance of the asso- 
ciation’s revised Pharmacist Care Claim Form to document ser- 
vices and facilitate payment for pharmacist care

DRUG ST@RE NEWS ° 


OTC SPECIAL REPORT 


UG SIRIKE NEWS 
2= PHARMACIST 


by Mike Troy 


t just a little past the midpoint 
A: the decade, the OTC mar- 

setplace is changing in ways 
that are nothing short of dramatic. 
For starters, look at the untold doc- 
tor visits that have been avoided by 
the OTC availability of products such 
as Imodium AD®, Monistat 7®, 
Tavist®, Aleve® and most recently 
Pepcid AC®. The Rx-to-OTC switch 
of these products’ active ingredients 
has given consumers new options 
and retailers new selling opportuni- 
ties. Meanwhile, innovation in 
dosage forms and delivery methods 
along with technological advance- 
ments have also presented con- 
sumers with self care alternatives 
that are more effective and easier to 
use. But more than the product as- 
sortment is changing. 

Consumer attitudes are also in 
flux as they are reportedly taking 
better care of themselves and self- 
medicating more often. They are also 
reading labels, buying store brands 
and asking questions of their 


JUNE 1995 


Exclusive survey: 
What consumers are 
thinking about OTCs 


pharmacist. 

Product distribution patterns have 
changed, too. Mass merchandisers 
grab a larger slice of the OTC pie 
every year, and supermarkets are 
more aggressively seeking OTC 
sales. Chain drug stores remain the 
preferred OTC outlet, but they face 
challenges. Many independent drug 
stores remain viable and serve a loy- 
al customer base even though their

behaviors, categories $3

consumers want more

OTC opportunities 


$29 


$33 


demise has long been predicted. 

Amid all this change, Drug Store 
News and Drug Store News For the 
Pharmacist put together a unique 
consumer study to examine topics 
such as: 

® where consumers prefer to shop 
for various OTC categories; 

® consumer attitudes toward the 
pharmacist’s advice on OTC use and 
whether or not they are willing to 

pay for that advice; 

® the extent to which consumers 
actually read product labeling; 

® what influences their decision 
to buy a brand vs. a private label, 
and 

® desirability of more prescrip- 
tion products being made available 
over-the-counter. 

Answers to these questions were re- 
vealed when more than 1,000 tele- 
phone calls were placed to a random 
sample of geographically dispersed 
households. Results of the survey, con- 
ducted by ICR Survey Research 
Group, were weighted to represent the 
national average based on the U.S. 
adult population. The key findings are 
disclosed on the pages that follow.

‘ Ps Senior vice president of FoxMeyer, Robert E. Davis resigned 
FoxMeyer KY Davis abruptly as president of APhA to avoid apparent conflicts 
« stemming from the association's new program to develop 
quits as APhA prez: pharmaceutical care training programs, computer software 
- - and related products. (See p. 25 for more on that.) Davis, who 
cites conflicts was installed in the APhA presidency in March, was recently 
named to head up FoxMeyer’s information management sub- 
sidiary, OmNex Health. Citing “external perceptions regard- 
ing my two positions,” Davis said, “I cannot have APhA's and 
my employer's intentions questioned because of the nature of 
their businesses.” APhA president-elect Calvin Knowlton was 
named to replace Davis. 


= - Ps The government’s widely-quoted Producer Price Index (PPI) 
Rx inflation is “has overstated drug price increases substantially since at 
least 1984,” auditors at the U.S. General Accounting Office 
exaggerated (GAO) told Congress. Much of the problem involves pre-1994 
sampling techniques which “under represented new and re- 
GAO concludes cently introduced drugs,” GAO said. Additionally, the index 
fails to reflect cost savings when consumers shift to cheaper 
generic drugs, or distinguish between drug product improve- 
ments and “pure price changes which constitute inflation.” 
Bottom line: GAO says prescription drug inflation has been 
exaggerated by at least 23 percent over the past decade. 
Ok A RR 


Although Pharmacy Direct Network (PDN) collected more 
PDN members may than $8 million in “charter membership” fees last year, by 
March, approximately 70 percent of that -- nearly $6 million-- 
get rebate when had been spent “to develop an infrastructure to enable PDN to 
- become competitive,” according to a “proxy statement” sent out 
sale is completed last month. Drug chains and independents who paid out $300 
per store will get back $147.23 within 90 days of PDN’s sale to 
American Stores, plus smaller ($41.31) annual “incentive pay- 
ments” from the chain each of the next three years. The deal is 
“iffy”, though: it must be approved by 75 percent of PDN’s 
membership, and those who do not agree to transfer their 
“provider agreements” to American Stores and remain in the 
network will get no payments. The vote is set for July 11. 


= While Pharmacy was digesting American store’s PDN offer, 

American okays the chain dropped a second bombshell: an agreement to sell 
part ownership of its year-old RxAmerica PBM company to 

a separate PBM Ciba’s Geneva generic subsidiary. The 35,000-store Rx Ameri- 

= ca network will be operated separately from PDN. The deal 

deal with Geneva marks the first move by a pharmacuetical maker to vertically 
integrate into a drug-chain owned PBM, and it will almost cer- 
tainly come uner federal antitrust scrutiny.

ee = COVER STORY 


Certified technicians: After your job? 


Continued from page 13 

play an enhanced role in patient care 
outcomes.” Apparently certification 
was never meant to be a substitute 
for on-the-job training. In fact, refer- 
ring to the state certification tests, 
Meyers claims, “Those just out of 
school have not done as well as those 
with at least a few years of experi- 
ence. To prepare for the test, you 
should be a working technician with 
some sort of on-the-job training.” 

Alice Foust, CPhT, president of 
the American Association of Pharma- 
cy Technicians (AAPT), agrees that 
on-the-job experience is vital and 
even goes one step further. “I think 
that certification should be mandato- 
ry, not voluntary, but we need stan- 
dard educational guidelines.” By 
standard, Foust is referring to a com- 
bined program of “standard educa- 
tion plus six months to a year of job 
experience prior to taking the certifi- 
cation exam.” 

Another objection to certification 
is the fact that there is no mecha- 
nism in place to revoke a certifica- 
tion. Carmen Catizone, executive di- 
rector of NABP, is concerned that “if 
a certified technician doesn’t demon- 
strate that they are capable on the 
job, or if they are dismissed because 
they were involved in drug diverting, 
they still have that certificate. They 
can move to another state and con- 
tinue to practice. The process does 
not protect the public health.” 


What pharmacists think 


In a survey conducted by Drug 
Store News For the Pharmacist, over 
two-thirds of the pharmacists in our 
sample (64 percent) think that the 
nationwide testing and certification 
of pharmacy technicians is a good 
idea. (See chart above.) Ken Smith, 
R.Ph., practices at a Wal-Mart in 
Ruskin, Fla., where technicians are 
used extensively. In fact there are 
three pharmacists and four techs at 
this particular store. Smith says, 
“Certification ensures quality techni- 
cians. You know they are well- 
trained, so they can take some of the 
responsibility from the pharmacist.” 

ICPH’s Meyers says, “Certification 
gives the employer a level of comfort. 
The pharmacist can count on the fact 
that a certified technician has a 
standardized level of knowledge.” 
Certification can also reverse the 


Tech national 
certification is a step 
in the right direction 


(% of pharmacists responding) 


Strongly disagree 
10% Strongly agree 


31% 


Somewhat 
disagree 
13% 


Neutral 
13% 


Somewhat agree 
33% 


technician’s image as a glorified 
clerk. Meyers adds, “Certification 
tells the employer that the tech has 
made a commitment to their career. 
It is not just a job.” 

Mike Leake, R.Ph., of Leake Phar- 
macy in Danville, Ky., gives the pro- 
gram an enthusiastic thumbs up. “I’m 
in favor of it. No matter who you hire, 
they have to learn how your particu- 


lar practice works. As an employer, if 


I’m looking for someone to be a tech, it 
would be nice to have someone that 
you know has the education and basic 
understanding. You know the learn- 
ing curve is going to be shorter.” 


What techs think 


There are two pharmacists and 
two full-time techs plus a part-timer 
at Leake Pharmacy. The technicians 
are not certified to date. Helen 
Tussey, who is one of those techni- 
cians, would like to be certified. “I 
feel it would add value to the title. I 
would take any training that would 
make me a better technician.” 
Tussey believes that being certified 
would add credibility to the position. 
“Wearing something that says you 
are a certified tech would make the 
customer feel you are more well- 
trained and qualified for the job.” 

Harrell Gillis Jr., CPhT, is vice 
president of AAPT. As a technician 


for 24 years, he says, “It is very im- 


portant to be certified. It shows your 
employer that you have the basic 
knowledge to work in the field.” 


But do techs want your job? 


There is an emotional aspect to the 
certification issue that NABP’s Cati- 
zone talked about. “Practitioners see 
certification as a challenge for their 
jobs. Similar to the nursing crisis, if 
technicians can perform some of the 
same functions as pharmacists at a 
lower expense, there is concern that 
technicians will be replacing them.” 

Leake does not feel that techs are 
a threat to his practice. “We are a 
more hands-on type of practice with 
specialty niches. If a tech can take 
care of all the mechanical things, 
then it allows me to have more pa- 
tient interaction. Some pharmacists 
are happy just counting and pouring, 
so for them I can see how tech certifi- 
cation would be a scary prospect. I 
don’t want my pharmacy to be like 
an assembly line.” 

Technician Tussey says she is not 
interested in the pharmacist’s job. 
“The tech’s role is everything up to 
the point where the pharmacist 
counsels the patient. I would never 
want to be the pharmacist.” /Editor’s 
Note: Tussey confided to Drug Store 
News For the Pharmacist that, as a 
result of this interview, she planned 
to talk to her employer about becom- 
ing certified. In a separate interview, 
Leake told DrSN/FTP that he would 
consider having his techs certified.] 

Wal-Mart’s Smith also rejects the 
notion that technicians are a threat 
to his job. “It is an opportunity to 
better the profession. Our public im- 
age is bound to improve because 
technicians will give pharmacists 
more time for counseling.” 

MPA’s Wagenknecht says techni- 
cians are not the pharmacist’s foe. 
“Technicians are the pharmacists’ 
biggest assets. Unless they change the 
way they practice, pharmacists will be 
replaced by nurses and other health 
care providers, not by techs. Techni- 
cians are the means to an end of tran- 
sitioning the pharmacists role to pa- 
tient care—if they are used properly.” 

Economics 

Among other objections to certifi- 

cation is the concern that technicians 


who are certified will demand higher

Pharmacy techs: Training for your job? 


Continued from page 14 
salaries. In a survey conducted last 
fall by the Michigan Pharmacist 
Journal, technicians in hospital and 
independent retail settings earned 
similar wages whether certified or 
not. The biggest discrepancy was re- 
ported for techs working in the 
chains. Certified chain techs earned 
at least a dollar more per hour than 
their non-certified counterparts. 
Therese Kirklys, R.Ph., is president 
of Pharmstaff, Ltd., a nationwide em- 
ployment agency specializing in full- 
and part-time drug store personnel. 
She reports that technicians earn be- 
tween $7 and $13 per hour, depend- 
ing on the individual’s experience as 
well as the practice site. Certified 
techs tend to earn an average of 50 
cents more per hour. At this time,

In development... 


Zeneca Pharmaceuticals has filed 
an NDA for Arimidex® (anastro- 
zole) tablets for the treatment of ad- 
vanced breast cancer in post- 
menopausal women who have 
progressed following therapy with 
tamoxifen. Arimidex is a non- 
steroidal selective oral aromatase 
inhibitor. Inhibition of aromatase 
reduces the production of estrogen. 


Famciclovir, (Famvir®, Smith- 
Kline Beecham) has been shown in 
two clinical trials to treat all symp- 
toms of recurrent genital herpes, 
including duration of viral shed- 
ding, time to complete lesion heal- 
ing and time to resolution of symp- 
toms. The drug was approved by 
the FDA in 1994 for the treatment 
of shingles (herpes zoster) and is 
approved in the U.K. for the treat- 


Kirklys says, “There is no difference 
in the demand for certified or non-cer- 
tified technicians. Most employers are 
interested in experience.” 


Two sides of the same certificate 


The technicians we spoke with, 
certified or not, have stated that they 
are not interested in taking over the 
pharmacist’s job. Most pharmacists 
we polled are in favor of certification 
and believe it adds value to the pro- 
fessional image of the pharmacy. 

It is the associations who sit on 
opposite sides of the fence on the 
certification issue. Or do they? 
Those opposing national certifica- 
tion agree that the educational val- 
ue of the program is extremely im- 
portant, but site-specific training is 
more valuable. Those supporting na-

tional certification agree that there 
is no substitute for job experience, 
but that a standard of knowledge is 
most practical. 

Then what’s the big deal about a 
piece of paper? Apparently, the an- 
swer depends on which side of the 
fence you're on. FTP

NEW YORK — American Soci- 
ety of Hypertension (ASH) work- 
ing committee recommendations 
on home blood pressure monitor- 
ing were offered at its recent 10th 
Annual Meeting in New York City 
by Thomas G. Pickering, M.D., 
Ph.D., professor of medicine, Cor- 
nell Medical Center, New York, 
New York. 

Dr. Pickering offered the follow- 
ing recommendations: 

® While aneroid sphygmo- 
manometers are inexpensive and 
reliable, the calibration needs to 
be checked regularly. 

® Electronic devices may be 
good, but only two have been 
properly validated as accurate: 
Omron HEM 706 and Omron 
HEM 403. 


Home biood pressure 
monitoring recommendations 


® Care must be taken that cuff 
sizes are correct for the patient-- 
appropriate sizes for obese patients 
may not be available, and finger 
cuff devices should not be used. 

® Patients should have their 
techniques checked by a trained 
professional. 

Further, self-monitoring is rec- 
ommended for most hypertensive 
patients, according to Pickering, 
because of four potential advan- 
tages: 

1. It can distinguish between 
sustained and “white coat hyper- 
tension”; 

2 It can assess response to anti- 
hypertensive agents; 

3. It can improve patient com- 
pliance; and 

4. It may reduce costs.

A = NEWS TRENDS §6©— 


American Stores’ acquisition of Pharmacy Direct Network 

PDN members OK (PDN) won approval by an overwhelming 87 percent of the 
o 8 drug chain and independent pharmacy members voting on 

American Stores the chain's offer last month. PDN's Alexandria, Va., offices 

were immediately relocated to American Stores’ headquar- 
buy-out offer ters in Salt Lake City, and chain officials assured the net- 
work’s 32,000 retail pharmacy members that PDN “will re- 
main a viable alternative” to traditional pharmacy benefit 
management (PBMs) organizations with selective member- 
ship policies. American Stores will shell out less than $9 mil- 
lion for PDN—a bargain considering CibaGeneva just agreed 
to pay $100 million for half ownership in American Stores’ 
own year-old, 35,000 member PBM, Rx America. 


| 


| The nation’s pharmacists would receive almost instanta- 

| APhA calls for neous notice of “important drug interactions and product 

| s recalls” under an innovative new “on-line electronic” warn- 

| electronic FDA ing system that the American Pharmaceutical Association 

= (APhA) is urging the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) 

link to pharmacy to develop. During meetings with FDA’ers, APhA officials 

also urged the agency to involve pharmacists “as re- 
searchers to assess the effectiveness of new drugs in actual 
practice.” 


| 
| ~ The FDA would be obliged to expedite reviews of new drug 
Evidence of ah applications (NDAs) for products already approved in Eu- 
| a Se rope, under a new FDA reform plan proposed by the Pharma- 
| U.S. drug lag » ceutical Research and Manufacturers of America (PhRMA). 
Underscoring need for such streamlining: a new PhRMA sur- 
Maybe and, vey identifying more than three dozen prescription medicines 
currently available to patients abroad, but still stuck in the 
maybe not FDA's gum-footed review process. A politically correct 
PhRMA footnote states that the new survey “does not prove 
that the FDA is slower than regulatory agencies in other 
countries, or that there is a drug lag.” Hmmmm. 


= 
FTC chief eyes A new antitrust task force being assembled by Federal Trade 
= Commission (FTC) chairman Robert Pitofsky is expected to 
| stricter R-P Act recommend far more vigorous FTC enforcement of the Robin- 
son-Patman law against predatory pricing activities by drug 
enforcement chains against independent pharmacies. “We are very inter 
ested in the notion of preserving the independents whether 
it’s in...the drug business, or any other,” he told a recent re- 
tail industry meeting. “I think there are cases in which power 
buyers can disadvantage smaller independents through their 
buying power,” Pitofsky said. “I am very much opposed to 


[antitrust] non-enforcement as a regulatory philosophy.”

DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, AUGUST 1995 


COVER STORY siamese 


Sorting through the GATT puzzie 


Where generics 
fit into the 
picture 


by Ken Rankin - Value of drug patent expirations 1994-2001 


he past year has been a partic- 

ularly turbulent period for the 

generic drug market, as the in- 
dustry found itself facing political, 
regulatory and scientific challenges 
that threatened to delay the intro- 
duction of generic competition for 
scores of major brand name pharma- 
ceuticals. But the marketers of 
generic drugs scored some victories 
of their own during the past year, 
and industry leaders are expressing 
a strong sense of optimism about the _ 
future of generic pharmaceuticals in 1994 
the emerging health care system of 
the 21st century. Much of that opti- 
mism is grounded in the conviction 
that the demand for lower-priced 


Based on 1993 U.S. sales in $ millions 


1995 


Source: IMS America 


generic equivalents will rise steadily 

in the increasingly cost-conscious 

managed health care environment. 
“The next 40 months or so will be 


one of the most explosive periods of 


growth ever for the generic drug in- 
dustry,” IVAX president John Klein 
predicted during his inaugural ad- 
dress as chairman of the Generic 
Pharmaceutical Industry Association 
(GPIA) earlier this year. 

Independent growth forecasts from 
researchers who track the pharma- 
ceutical market tend to support such 
optimistic projections. A recent analy- 
sis by Dillon, Reed & Co. predicts that 
combined sales of generic drugs by re- 
tail and hospital pharmacies will 
jump 26 percent this year alone, to 
nearly $7.6 billion. (See chart at 
right.) By the year 2001, the re- 
searchers estimate that the demand 
for generics will jump to $11.9 billion. 

Even more optimistic forecasts are 
coming from analysts at Gemini Con- 
sulting, who expect that “within a 
few years” generics will explode to 
capture 50 percent of all U.S. phar- 


maceutical dollar sales, and 80 per- 
cent of unit sales. 

In part, these bullish forecasts re- 
flect the steady increase in the pro- 
portion of prescriptions filled generi- 
cally. In step with the growing

son % 


i poh Sa oe 


The U.S. generic drug market 
Retail & hospital generic drug sales in $ million 


influence of managed care, IMS 
America reports that the percentage 
of all U.S. prescriptions filled generi- 
cally has increased from 32 percent 
in 1990 to 42 percent last year. Sig-

DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, AUGUST 1995 


COVER STORY 


Where generics fit into the picture 


Continued from page 17 

nificantly, however, there’s reason to 
believe that the generic drug indus- 
try’s best customers are in the retail 
segment, not in HMOs or mail order. 
(See charts below.) 

An FTP study published in April 
found that among drug chains, 43 
percent of all prescriptions are now 
being filled with generic equivalents. 
And among independent pharmacies, 
nearly half (48 percent) of all scripts 
are filled generically. 

Consumers themselves are, at 
least in part, responsible for the 
growing appetite for lower-cost 
generic pharmaceuticals. Nine-out-of- 
10 pharmacists responding to our 
own reader survey said today’s cus- 
tomers are not only more knowledge- 
able about generics, but more likely 


Patients want doctor intervention 


to request generic substitution than 
those just five years ago. (See pg. 27.) 
Meanwhile, a separate FTP survey 
of consumers found that over three- 
quarters of the respondents said they 
would react positively if their phar- 
macist suggested a generic substitute. 
Indeed, over half of all consumers ex- 
pect savings of more than 20 percent 
when the pharmacist dispenses a 
generic drug. (See charts pg. 19.) 


Patents expire 


While these factors certainly con- 
tribute to a positive outlook for the 
industry, the chief reason for opti- 
mism about the future of the generic 
drug market is the long list of major 
brand name pharmaceuticals sched- 
uled to lose patent exclusivity over 
the next few years. (See table pg. 
22.) An analysis by IMS America 
found that at least 36 brand name 
drugs, with combined U.S. sales of 
more than $4 billion, are scheduled 
to go off patent during the next 17 
months alone. 

Moreover, by the year 2015, offi- 
cials at IMS America report, some 
$20 billion worth of brand name 
pharmaceutical products will lose 
patent protection and become candi- 
dates for generic competition. (See 
chart pg. 17.) 


GATT complicates 


But the best laid plans of the in- 
dustry to introduce generic competi- 
tion for many of the drugs scheduled 
to lose patent exclusivity were 
thrown into turmoil earlier this year 


Source: Drug Store News For the Pharmacist, Food Marketing institute. 


as the complexities of the new Gener- 
al Agreement on Tariffs and Trade 
(GATT) treaty came back to haunt 
the industry. In addition to breaking 
down tariffs and other barriers to in- 
ternational trade, the GATT treaty 
requires the U.S. to abandon its long- 
standing practice of granting phar- 
maceutical manufacturers 17-year 
patents from the time of a drug’s ap- 
proval. Instead, the treaty mandates 
the system used by other nations of 
granting 20-year patents from the 
time they are applied for. 
, | 
The new rules were designed to 
cover products submitted for patent 
approval after June 8, 1995, but be- 
fore the ink was dry on the treaty, 
generic industry leaders and congres- 
sional legislators discovered an 
Continued on page 19 


..And supermarket pharmacies 
are slipping 


% of supermarket Rx $ sales trem generics 


DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, AUGUST 1995 


Where generics fit into the picture 


Continued from page 18 
unanticipated “loophole” that could 
delay the introduction of generic 
competition for scores of additional 
drugs already on the market. 

“We were blindsided by this devel- 
opment,” GPIA’s Klein admits 

At issue is whether products 
whose patents took effect prior to 
June 8 will necessarily gain addition- 
al patent life as a result of GATT. 

Congress envisioned allowing com- 
panies to introduce generic versions 


of patented products on schedule, if 


they had made a “substantial invest- 
ment” in product development in an- 
ticipation of the original patent expi- 
ration date, and they agreed to pay 


What consumers expect to save 
with generics 


Source: FTP/ICR survey research 


“equitable remuneration” to the 
patent holder. 

Unfortunately for the generic drug 
industry, however, Congress failed to 
specifically address the generic drug 
approval process in passing legisla 
tion implementing GATT. Brand 
name drug manufacturers argued 
that the 1984 Waxman-Hatch law 
governing generic approvals super- 
sedes the new GATT legislation, and 
that even drugs whose patents had 
been extended under that law should 
qualify for GATT patent extensions. 
Generic drug industry leaders quick- 
ly took the offensive, charging that 
the GATT “loophole” could have “a 
significant, negative impact on U.S 
health care cost containment efforts.” 


Future extensions 


An economic study conducted for 
the industry by University of Min- 


COVER STORY 


nesota PRIME Institute director 
Stephen Schondelmeyer identified at 
least 105 brand name drugs currently 
on the market that would be in line to 
receive such “unearned” GATT patent 
extensions. 

Schondelmeyer’s study, which was 


jointly funded by grants from the Na- 


tional Association of Pharmaceutical 
Manufacturers (NAPM) and the Na- 
tional Pharmaceutical Alliance 
(NPA), found that “the American 
public will be forced to pay a conserv- 
atively-estimated $6 billion in higher 
drug prices due to windfall brand 
name patent extensions” granted 
under GATT. 

Citing the PRIME Institute’s find- 
ings in an appeal for intervention by 
the U.S. Patent and Trademark Of- 
fice, NAPM president Robert Mi- 
lanese and NPA executive director 
Chris Sizemore warned that “the 
new GATT treaty will produce un- 
necessary and burdensome economic 
hardships on American consumers.” 

The Patent Office, however, ap- 
peared unlikely to intercede on be- 
half of the generic drug industry, and 
the action shifted to the Food and 
Drug Administration where that 
agency was agonizing over a “citi- 
zen’s petition” from Glaxo arguing 
that generics should not be approved 
for brand name drugs during the 
GATT-extended patent period 

Faced with the threat of a lawsuit, 
FDA ruled in favor of Glaxo, and 
sparked a fresh firestorm of criticism 
in Congress and throughout the 
generic drug industry 


Restoring fairness 


Charging that FDA’s action will 
result in the “unjustified enrich 
ment” of several major brand name 
drug firms, Sen. David Pryor 
promptly announced plans “to intro- 
duce legislation to correct this in- 
equity % 

For their part, NAPM’s Milanese 
and NPA’s Sizemore issued a joint 
statement expressing “shock” at the 
FDA’s “anti-consumer interpreta- 
tion” of GATT, while GPIA president 
Lewis Engman voiced “outrage” at 
Kessler's decision “to give big drug 
companies windfall profits which will 
be paid for by American consumers. 

“I am surprised that this Adminis- 
tration would force consumers to pay 
dramatically higher prices for drugs 


DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, AUGUST 1995 


at a time when health care costs 
have become such a national con- 
cern,” he added. “I predict that this 
matter is far from settled.” 

Engman’s prediction proved to be 
on target, as less than two weeks lat- 
er the generic drug industry’s GATT 
saga took another sharp plot twist. 
In an abrupt reversal of its earlier 
position, the patent office ruled that 
pharmaceuticals whose patent lives 
had already been extended under the 
Waxman-Hatch law are not entitled 
to an additional extension under 
GATT. The patent office decision ef- 
fectively took much of the sting out of 
the FDA’s ruling by denying GATT 
patent extensions for at least 94 cur- 


Here's how patients react to 
generic substitutes 


Source: FTP/ICR survey research 


rently marketed drugs—a move that 
generic industry leaders say will 
save American consumers more than 
$4 billion in medication costs. 

The bottom line, according to 
GPIA's Engman: “After months of 
speculation that the GATT amend- 
ments would have a devastating im- 
pact on the entry of generic medi- 
cines, it turns out” that “very few 
existing patents for new drugs will 
be extended at all.” 

The two prime exceptions are big 
ones, however: Bristol-Myers 
Squibb’s Capoten® and Glaxo’s Zan- 
tac®—drugs with combined sales of 
more than $2 billion annually whose 
patents had been scheduled to expire 
this year. Now—unless shortened by 
a flurry of expected court chal- 
lenges—Capoten will retain patent 
exclusivity until February 1996, and 
Zantac will stay on patent into 1997.

Pharmacists say patients 
savvy about generics 


onsumer attitudes toward generic 

drugs are changing—and that’s 
good news for the industry. According 
to en exclusive new Drug Store News 
For the Pharmacist (FTP) survey, to- 
day’s prescription drug purchasers 
are becoming increasingly knowl- 
edgeable about generic pharmaceuti- 
cals, and they’re more likely to ask 
their pharmacist to dispense a gener- 
ic equivalent. 

But the news is not all good for the 
generic drug industry. The F7P sur- 
vey results suggest that while to- 
day’s consumers are 


Asked te compare today’s con- 
sumers with those of five years ago, 
better than 9-out-of-10 pharmacists 
said today’s prescription customers 
are more knowledgeable about gener- 
ic drugs. 

An independent pharmacist from 
Wisconsin suggested that the public’s 
heightened awareness of generics is 
relative. “At least not as many cus- 
tomers are calling them ‘genetic’ or 
‘geriatric’ drugs,” she said. 

Although they agreed that the 
public is more knowledgeable about 


ask for generics then they were in 
1990. In contrast, 100 percent of the 
independents told F7P researchers 
that consumer requests for generic 
equivalents are more common today. 


Pro-active consumers 


The FTP pharmacist survey also 
uncovered evidence that today’s pre- 
scription customers are becoming in- 
creasingly aggressive in their efforts 
to obtain lower-cost generic drugs. 
By a solid 2-to-1 margin, the respon- 
dents said consumers are more likely | 
to ask the pharmacist | 


more sophisticated 
about generics, they’re 
at the same time skep- 
tical about their safety 
and effectiveness. 

To get a fix on how 
the public’s attitudes 
toward generic drugs 
have changed, we sur- 
veyed a cross-section of 
FTP readers from 
across the country in 
all retail practice set- 
tings. As a benchmark, 
we asked pharmacists 
to compare the cus- 


Some consumers may have been 
turned off to generic drugs during the 
FDA scandals of the late 1980s, but a 


new Drug Store News For the 


Pharmacist survey suggests that 
today’s prescription customers are 
warming toward generic equivalents 


“to contact the pre- 
scriber for authoriza- 
tion to dispense a low- 
er cost medication.” 
The belief that to- 
day’s customers are 
more likely to request 
such pharmacist inter- 
vention was strongest 
among independents 
(73 percent vs. 54 per- 
cent of chain practi- 
tioners) and among 
pharmacists with 10 
or more years experi- 
ence (69 percent vs. 46 


tomer attitudes toward 


generics today vs. those 
of five years ago. We expected a 


somewhat more positive view of 
generics than in 1990—the peak of 


the generic drug scandals—and 
those expectations were confirmed by 
the results.

pointed out that consumers still have

greatly reducing their prescription

stronger appetite for lower cost

very rarely happens,” one said. 
There was little disagreement 
among pharmacists about whether 
today’s customers are more likely to

Pharmacist recommendations 


“I’m recommending generics 
whenever possible to my cash-paying 
customers, as well as to my third- 
party and Medicaid patients,” a S.C. 
independent said. 

Not surprisingly, the survey sug- 
gests that much of the public’s grow- 
ing interest in generics is being fu- 
eled by concerns about prescription 
drug costs. As one Alabama indy put 
it, the “skyrocketing prices” charged 
by drug manufacturers “are prompt- 
ing more and more of my customers 
to ask for generics.” 

Indeed, 72 percent of the pharma- 
cists surveyed said that “compared to 
consumers five years ago, today’s 
prescription drug purchasers are 
more likely to complain about high 
drug prices.” One in five, however, 
reported no change in the frequency 
of consumer drug price complaints, 


Consumers demand more > 
information 


while 7 percent said the public is less 
likely to complain about prescription 
costs today. “We get complaints from 
people who pay cash,” said one Find- 
lay, Ohio, retail pharmacist, “but so 
many people have insurance cards 
today and they don’t have a clue 
about the cost of prescriptions.” 

The survey also revealed an un- 
dercurrent of pharmacist discontent 
with the generic dispensing and re- 
imbursement policies of pharmacy 
benefit management organizations. 


Third-party interference 


“We really resent being told by 
some third-party plans to substi- 
tute ‘non-AB’ rated drugs,” said 
one retail pharmacy owner in the 
Midwest. “These policies create a 
lot of friction between consumers 
trying to save money and pharma- 
cists trying to make proper thera- 
peutic decisions.” 

“I’d like to see more insurance 
companies set patient co-pays lower 
for generics,” said another. “One of 
the largest employers in our town of- 
fers a drug benefit with a single co- 
pay, and as a result all the employ- 
ees ask for more expensive 
brand-name drugs. No wonder Amer- 
ican health care is so expensive.” 

Although FTP’s reader survey 
found that prescription drug pur- 
chasers are warming to generics as a 
means to hold down costs, the results 
indicate that today’s consumers are 
not blindly accepting generic substi- 
tutes. More than 4-in-10 pharmacists 
(43 percent) said their customers are 
“more likely to question the quality 
or safety of generic drugs,” while 
only 20 percent said the public is less 
suspicious of generics today. Over a 
third (37 percent) reported no differ- 
ence in the public’s tendency to ques- 
tion generic quality or safety. 

Chain pharmacists were particu- 
larly likely to encounter patients 
skeptical of generics. A solid 64 per- 
cent of chain pharmacists said their 
customers raise more questions 
about generics today, while only 7 
percent said the public was more 
likely to question these products five 
years ago. Independents, meanwhile, 
were split down the middle, with 29 
percent reporting more customer 
questions about generic safety and 
quality, and 29 percent reporting 
less.

Source: Drug Store News For the Pharmacist 


Pharmacists on generics 


For their part, most of the phar- 
macists responding to our survey— 
chain and independent alike—ex- 
pressed clearly positive views about 
generic drugs. 

Despite the apparent rise in con- 
sumer skepticism about generic drug 
quality, a pharmacist from Bullard, 
Texas, said there’s “no question” 
about the acceptance of generics by 
his customers. “The only ones who 
demand brand name drugs today are 
the ones with insurance cards.” 

Others expressed similar opinions: 
“Generic drugs are equivalent,” said 
an Atlanta, Ga., independent. “There’s 
no justification for the higher prices 
charged for brand name products.” 

“I consider generics a good, high 
quality way to save money for my pa- 
tients,” a chain pharmacist from 
Madison, Wis., added. 

“I don’t dispense anything that I 
won’t use myself, and I take generics 
whenever I have the choice,” said an 
independent from Winnfield, La. 

“I feel comfortable recommending 
generics to our customers,” a chain 
pharmacist from Farmville, N.C., 
told FTP. “Very rarely does a cus- 
tomer complain about the generic not 
working as well as the brand.” 

That view was not universal, how- 
ever. “I’m seeing more people who 
come into my store and tell me that 
the brand name product just works 
better,” said a chain pharmacist from 
Cobleskill, N.Y.

Drug patent immortality 


Can generics crack the “off-patent” market? 


L order to realize their full market 
potential, the makers of generic 
druys will have to overcome a num- 
ber of other challenges in today’s in- 
creasingly competitive marketplace. 
Not the least of these is the ingenuity 
employed by brand name pharmaceu- 
tical manufacturers who are finding 
ways to protect their big money prod- 
ucts from generic competition, even 
after their patents expire 

An analysis by IMS America iden- 
tified at least 20 brand 1 1e 
drugs—each with U.S. sales exceed- 
ing $100 million a year—that con- 
tinue to enjoy market exclusivity de- 
spite losing their patent protection 
(See table pg. 36.)

sons for the absence of generic com- 
petitors for these products, industry 
leaders have accused the manufac- 
turers of some off-patent pharmaceu-

cists said their generic comfort level

lent today.’

the future “if brand name manufac-

Opposing state anti-generic legislation 


joining forces with pharmacist 
groups across the country to beat 
back a rash of state legislative pro- 
posals that they say are threatening 
to limit generic substitution, narrow 
pharmacist product selection rights 
and deny consumers access to the 
most cost-effective medicines. Much 
of the industry’s activity is being co- 
ordinated by Susan Mora, the 
Generic Pharmaceutical Industry 
Association (GPIA) director of state 


( — drug manufacturers are 


affairs, and an outspcken critic of 


what she considers a

cals. According to the Pfizer petition, 
new ground rules are warranted by 
“well-recognized scientific principles 
which establish that differences in 
the release mechanisms used in con- 
trolled-release drug products result 
in non-identical dosage forms and 
such differences in dosage form are 
potentially significant to public 
health and safety.” 

Although the changes sought by 


Pfizer “would impact the use of 


many existing generic medicines,” 
GPIA officials contend that the com- 


In contrast, “GPIA’s position is 
that there is no medical reason for 
this legislation,” Mora says. 

Despite generic industry objec- 
tions, Connecticut lawmakers enact- 
ed Pfizer’s bill last year, and a simi- 
lar measure passed the California 
legislature, only to be vetoed by Gov- 
ernor Pete Wilson after intense lob- 
bying by the state’s pharmacists. 

This year “dosage form” language 
came under consideration in 10 more 
legislatures across the country from 
Georgia to Colorado, and has so far 

been defeated in

undermine’ generic 
product selection laws.
