“Dosage form” bills 


Near the top of the generic drug 
industry’s list of concerns is the flur- 
ry of state legislative proposals that 
would limit the ability of pharma- 
cists to substitute generic equiva- 
lents that use a delivery mechanism 
different from the brand name ver- 


sion. “By designating mechanisms of 


drug delivery as separate ‘dosage 
forms’, these bills would, in effect, 
allow particular brand name compa- 
nies to extend their monopolies be- 
yond the life of their drug patents,” 
Mora contends. 

This issue surfaced as a concern 
for generic manufacturers and phar- 
macists nearly two years ago when 
Pfizer Inc. petitioned the Food and 
Drug Administration (FDA) for 
changes in the agency’s approval 
standards for generic pharmaceuti- 


pany’s “specific intent in filing this 
petition appears to have been to pro- 
tect from substitution its [$1 billion- 
a-year] controlled-release nifedipine 
drug product, Procardia® XL, which 
employs a patented oral osmotic 
pump delivery system.” 

The FDA is stil) considering that 
petition, but Pfizer isn’t waiting for 
an answer from the agency. Instead, 
the company is seeking legislation in 
a number of states that would re- 
strict generic substitution for drugs 
with different delivery mechanisms 
“The language used in these state 
bills is sufficiently vague that it 
could be interpreted to prohibit the 
substitution of not only controlled- 
release products, but also other 
generic medicines which have been 
deemed bioequivalent by the FDA 
and by many state scientific bodies,” 
GPIA said. 

For their part, advocates of these 
bills contend that such changes in 
the generic substitution ground 
rules are needed to reduce pharma- 
cist confusion following recent 
changes in drug delivery technology 


DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, AUGUST 1995 


providers and Medic- 
aid administrators” to discourage en- 
actment of such legislation. 


Prescription pad formats 


The generic drug industry is also 
battling a number of more subtle 
forms of state restrictions on product 
substitution, including rules on the 
books in more than a dozen states 
allowing physicians to use two-line 
prescription order pads. 

Since the “do not substitute” line 
is typically on the lower right corner 
of the pad, critics of this system say 
there is a tendency among pre- 
scribers to unconsciously block the 
use of generics when they sign the 
script. According to GPIA, “with a 
two-line format, many substitutions 
are not made simply due to the 
physician’s signing habits.” 

To increase generic substitution, at 
least 34 states have adopted rules 
prohibiting two-line prescription pads 
and requiring a format that forces the 
prescriber to make a conscious effort 
when instructing pharmacists to dis- 
pense more costly brand name prod- 

Continued on page 42 


41 


COVER STORY 


Opposing state anti-generic legislation 


Continued from page 41 

ucts. “The resulting experience has 
been a significant increase in generic 
utilization, and thus prescription cost 
savings,” GPIA said. 

In 1987, after New York replaced 
its two-line system with one requir- 
ing prescribers to handwrite that a 
brand is “medically necessary,” a 
study by the Albany College of Phar- 
macy found that generic dispensing 
in the state had increased 45 per 
cent. What’s more, “in the seven 
months after the enactment of the 
one-line system, the state had saved 
approximately $95 million in pre- 
scription drug expenditures,” GPIA 
contends. 

The failure of some states to follow 
New York’s lead has created some po- 
tential reimbursement problems for 
pharmacists, according to Mora. This 
is because federal Medicaid rules 
deny reimbursement for the full cost 
of more expensive brand-name drugs, 
unless the prescriber indicates on the 
prescription form in his or her hand- 
writing that the brand is medically 
necessary. “States currently employ- 
ing a two-line prescription format are 
not in compliance with this Medicaid 
rule, and pharmacists in these 
states... run the risk of not being re- 
imbursed by Medicaid” simply be- 
cause the physician’s signature ap- 
pears in the wrong corner, she said. 

Legislation to abolish the two-line 
prescription pad system was intro- 
duced in at least six states this year, 
including Rhode Island where a bill 
requiring prescribers to write “brand 
medically necessary” in order to deny 
substitution was recently signed by 
the governor. In addition, West Vir- 
ginia lawmakers approved changes 
in that state’s Pharmacy Practice Act 
requiring prescribers in that state to 
handwrite “brand medically neces- 
sary” when seeking to block substitu- 
tion on a Medicaid prescription. 

Meanwhile, in Hawaii, the gover- 
nor vetoed legislation passed this 
session that would have repealed 
that state’s existing requirement 
that prescribers handwrite “brand 
medically necessary” in order to pro- 
hibit the use of a generic equivalent. 


Prior approval 
for brand name drugs 


In an effort to rein in runaway 


health care spending, a number of 


state Medicaid programs are at- 
tempting to reduce physician resis- 
tance to lower cost generic drugs by 
requiring them to obtain “prior ap- 
preval” from state officials for the 
use of brand name multi-source 
pharmaceuticals. In these states 
physicians who refuse to prescribe 
the lower-cost generic version are re- 
quired to contact Medicaid adminis- 
trators and explain why they feel the 
more costly brand name product is 
necessary. 

Under Pennsylvania’s year-old 
prior approval system, for exam- 
ple, physicians who insist on pre- 
scribing brand-name medicines 
must not only justify their deci- 
sion in advance, but also obtain a 
“prior approval identification 


Although prior 
approval systems 
are designed to 

reduce health care 
costs, they’re draw- 
ing stiff opposition 
from brand name 
drug makers who 
fear erosion of their 
market share. 


number” that must appear on the 
script. Florida’s even more restric- 
tive system prohibits physicians 
from overriding generic substitu- 
tion on Medicaid prescriptions un- 
less the product in question ap- 
pears on the state’s negative 
formulary. 

Although these prior approval sys- 
tems are designed to reduce health 
care costs, they’re drawing stiff opposi- 
tion in a number of states from brand 


name drug makers who fear erosion of 


their market share. Some physician 
groups are also critical of prior ap- 
proval requirements, which they view 
as unwarranted government interfer- 


ence with MD prescribing rights. 

Politically, state Medicaid restric- 
tions on the ability of physicians to 
veto generic substitution are also 
vulnerable to charges that such 
rules may create a two-tier system 
of health care in which medical as- 
sistance patients are forced to ac- 
cept inferior drug products. In re- 
sponse, GPIA representatives cite 
federal assurances to physicians 
that generics declared bioequivalent 
by the agency are indeed the equal 
of their more expensive brand name 
variations 

According to a 1988 FDA Talk 
Paper being circulated by the asso- 
ciation: “If a drug product is de- 
clared by the agency |FDA] to be 
therapeutically equivalent, a physi- 
cian, in managing a patient, can 
feel secure that authorizing substi- 
tution of the product for any other 
therapeutically equivalent drug 
will provide the same intended 
effect.” 


More proactive denial 


In addition, supporters of state 
Medicaid prior approval require- 
ments argue that they result in 
more rational prescribing by physi- 
cians. Prior approval rules make 
“the role of the prescriber in denying 
generic substitution more proac- 
tive,” GPIA contends. “As a result, 
requesting a substitution override is 
more likely to be limited to those in- 
stances when a legitimate medical 
reason exists for denying the use of 
a lower price, bioequivalent generic 
medicine.” 

The upshot, according to the asso- 
ciation, is a “significant savings” to 
Medicaid “without negatively im- 
pacting the quality of care offered to 
medical assistance recipients.” 

That view is underscored by a re- 
cent report from the Health and Hu- 
man Services Office of Inspector 
General (OIG) which urged federal 
Medicaid administrators to “identify 
and alert states to methods which 
would encourage the use of lower 
priced generic drug products in the 
Medicaid program.” The amount of 
savings at stake could be significant. 
According to the OIG’s study, re- 
stricting physician overrides for just 
37 high volume brand name drugs 
would reduce Medicaid drug program 
costs by $45 million annually. FTP

FEATURE 


A matter of trust for pharmacists 
Confronting new ethical dilemmas 


by Mike Troy 


any of today’s top film 
stars claim they don’t ac- 
knowledge press accounts 


of their work. After all, the rich 
and famous have little need for the 
affirmation and comfort of a movie 
critic’s favorable review 

It is a long way from a Holly- 
wood backlot to the pharmacy 
bench, but pharmacists are no 
strangers to the accolades of ador- 
ing masses. For the past decade, 
the nation has given the profession 
“two thumbs up” on honesty and 
ethics, an accomplishment even 
more noteworthy given an increas- 
ingly cynical population that seems 


to be growing more distrustful of 


institutions and government. 
Since 1985, pharmacists have 
been ranked by the Gallup Organi- 


zation as the profession with the 
highest standards of honesty and 
ethics. “America’s most trusted pro- 
fession,” is how pharmacy is fre- 
quently described. It is a reputa- 
tion secured through years of good 
deeds, accessibility and patient 
interaction. 

The favorable reviews bestowed 
upon pharmacy have not gone un- 
noticed by pharmacists who, unlike 
celebrities, do read their reviews. 
An impressive 62 percent of respon- 
dents to the latest Gallup survey 
rated the honesty and ethical stan- 
dard of pharmacists as high or very 
high. Pharmacists have an even 
higher opinion of themselves. An 
independent survey of 376 pharma- 
cists conducted by Drug Store News 
For the Pharmacist revealed that a 
whopping 93 percent rated the hon- 
esty and ethical standards of phar- 
macists as high or very high. This

Profiting from therapeutic 
interchange 


Pharmacy has, for the most part, 
escaped the type of scandal and 
negative publicity that seem to dog 
professions such as the clergy, 
politicians, lawyers and stockbro- 
kers. One of the biggest scandals in 
recent years to hit pharmacy, and 
cause pharmacists to rethink thei 
ethical standards, occurred several 
years ago. Remember those pre- 
scription drug marketing schemes 
which involved pharmacists being 
compensated for switching patients 
to alternative drug therapies even 
if there was no therapeutic or fi- 
nancial benefit to the patient? 
Those programs earned Miles, Up- 
john and Lederle fines totaling $1.2 
million and they have since disap- 
peared from the retail pharmacy 
landscape. 

“Efforts by FDA and other regu- 
latory agencies to control these pro- 
grams have made companies un- 
derstandably reluctant to pursue 


them,” said Lee Hill, president of 


Rescon Inc., a pharmaceutical mar- 
keting consulting firm. Potential 
damage to the pharmacist’s credi- 
bility “is one of the reasons phar- 
macists have not been eager to par-

ticipate and manufacturers have 
given up on these types of pro- 
grams.” According to Hill, manu- 
facturer efforts to gain market 
share are now more focused on giv- 
ing preferential sales terms 
to PBMs and managed care 
organizations. 


An impressive 93 
percent of 
pharmacists 
responding to FIP’s 
ethics survey rated 
the honesty and 
ethical standards of 
their peers as high 
or very high. 


Pharmacist opinions are mixed 
on participating in therapeutic in- 
terchange programs. Of those sur- 
veyed by Drug Store News For The 
Pharmacist, only 11 percent said it 
was acceptable to receive compen- 
sation for participation in a thera- 


peutic interchange program even if 


there was no monetary or thera- 
peutic benefit to the patient. Sixty 
percent said they would participate 
if there is a cost or therapeutic 
benefit to the patient. Thirty 
percent said participation is 
not appropriate under any 
circumstances. 


Abortion and suicide drugs 
controversy 


The availability of drugs such as 
RU 486, also known as the abortion 
pill, will give pharmacists new is- 
sues to concern themselves with 
Balancing personal beliefs with cor- 
porate responsibilities may put 
some practitioners on a tightrope. 
Referring specifically to RU 486, 
only 3.5 percent of survey respon- 


dents said they would definitely not 
dispense the drug. Most, 61 per- 
cent, said they would dispense if 
necessary. Another 26 percent said 
they would dispense the drug al- 
though they would rather not. 

Another ethical dilemma rapidly 
approaching is whether to dispense 
drugs that are intended for use in 
assisted suicides. Voters in the 
state of Oregon approved legisla- 
tion allowing assisted suicides in 
late 1994. 

“How will a pharmacist react to a 
prescription for a medication that 
will help a patient when used legit- 
imately, but could kill the patient 
when taken in excessive doses?” 
questioned Harvey Whitney Jr., a 
former pharmacy educator and 
publisher of a soon-to-be-available 
journal, Christianity and 
Pharmacy. Whitney said the publi- 
cation will be distributed to the 
roughly 2,000 members of the 
Christian Pharmacist Fellowship 
International. “We can expect phar- 
macists to be asked to provide in- 
formation about the names of the 
best drugs to use and the lethal 
dose for each. For the protection of 
pharmacists, one immediate action 
I propose is that state boards of 
pharmacy examine the legality of 
passing a regulation that requires 
prescribers to write the intended 
purpose on the prescription for 
every potentially lethal drug.’ 

Pharmacists have been very con- 
cerned about the Oregon legislation 
because it doesn’t clearly spell out 
the pharmacist’s role and a phar- 
macist could potentially fill a lethal 
prescription without knowledge of 
its purpose. Whether more states 
pass such legislation remains to be 
seen, but how Oregon’s pharma- 
cists deal with the issue will likely 
serve as a model for the rest of the 
nation 

“Clearly, there are pharmacists 
who have taken the position that 
they are not going to dispense 
drugs for use in assisted suicides, 
but it comes down to each individ- 
ual and how strongly they feel 
about it,” Whitney said. In some 
cases, the University of Michigan 
pharmacy school graduate specu- 
lated, pharmacists may have such 
strong feelings on the issue that 
there may be conflicts with 
employers. FTP

DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, SEPTEMBER 1995 


Re COVER STORY aero 


Consumers reeling from 


Rx sticker shock 


By Ken Rankin 


arlier this summer 

more than four dozen 

senior citizens from 
Minnesota crossed the bor- 
der into Canada to pur- 
chase prescription drugs at 
a Winnipeg pharmacy. The 
payoff from the Minnesota 
Senior Federation’s 12-hour 
bus ride: an opportunity to 
buy medicine such as Gly- 
buride 5 mg, 45 tablets for 
$6.44 (vs. $24.70 in the 
U.S.), and Ogen 0.625 mg, 
100s at $15.88 (instead of 
the $68.83 price at a com- 
munity pharmacy in Min- 
neapolis). 

According to the organiz- 
ers of the Rx shopping jun- 
ket, the purpose of the trip 
was not to demonstrate 
how cheap prescription drugs are in 
Canada—but how expensive those 
same products are on this side of the 
border. 

At the same time, they made it 
clear that they were not holding 
American pharmacists responsible 
for those high U.S. drug prices. “The 
message from the Senior Federation 
is not that we want to bypass Ameri- 
can pharmacists, because we trust 
their personal attention and we 
count on having pharmacies in our 
local communities,” they said. 

Not all U.S. consumers stung by 
rising prescription drug prices view 
the pharmacist as an innocent by- 
stander, however. Too often, phar- 
macists are the most convenient tar- 
get for consumer frustration and 
anger over the high cost of pharma- 
ceutical products. 

While there’s no consensus on the 
causes of pharmaceutical price infla- 
tion—or even whether drug prices are 
rising too fast—there’s no disputing 
one point: consumers who pay for 
their prescriptions out-of-pocket at the 
neighborhood pharmacy are hopping 
mad about what they consider the ex- 
cessive cost of many Rx medicines. 

A study conducted earlier this 


year by Drug Store News For The 
Pharmacist (FTP) suggests that re- 
tail pharmacy customers may have 
good reason to complain about pre- 
scription drug “sticker shock.” At the 
retail pharmacy level, prescription 
prices have increased from an aver- 
age of $11.62 per Rx in 1985 to 
$27.29 last year—a rise of 135 per- 
cent during the past decade alone 
more than triple the 42.2 percent in- 
crease in the Consumer Price Index 
for that same period. 

But while patients are paying sig- 
nificantly higher prices for their pre- 
scriptions these days, retail pharma- 
cy’ share of those Rx sales has been 
steadily shrinking. 


Shrinking Rx margins 


In contrast to the 1980s when re- 
tail gross margins for prescription 
drugs averaged 32 percent, the latest 
FTP study shows that Rx margins 
have dropped to 27.7 for indepen- 
dents and 24.3 percent for drug 
chains. And with operating costs on 
the increase, the net profit realized 
by retail pharmacy is averaging only 
pennies per prescription. 

NACDS vice president Laura 
Cranston drove that point home dur- 


DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, SEPTEMBER 1995 


ing recent testimony on 

behalf of the chain drug 

industry before state leg- 

islators in Olympia, 

Wash. On a typical $26 

prescription, “only 50 

cents, or 2 percent, is 

profit for the pharmacy— 

barely enough to stay in 

business,” she said. “The 

community retail phar- 

macist is clearly not re- 

sponsible for rising pre- 

scription drug prices.” 

The blame, according to 

Cranston, belongs square- 

ly with the producers of 

prescription medicines. 

Pharmaceutical “manu- 

facturer margins are up 

36 percent, and ours are 

down 36 percent between 

1970 and 1990—a full 72 

percent spread,” she said. 

For their part, repre- 

sentatives of the nation’s drug manu- 

facturers suggest that retail pharma- 

cists share much, if not most, of the 

responsibility for high prescription 

drug prices. Responding to public 

criticism about manufacturer price 

increases, officials at the Pharmaceu- 

tical Research and Manufacturers of | 

America (PhRMA) said that “con- 

sumers can benefit from comparison 

shopping” among pharmacies because 

“retail prices vary at a rate that 
dwarfs the rate of drug inflation.” 


Retail Rx prices 


A survey conducted earlier this 
year by New York City’s Department 
of Consumer Affairs, for example, 
found that prices charged for pre- 
scription drugs by the city’s pharma- 
cies varied by as much as 44 percent, 
PhRMA said. A separate Rx pricing 
study “found that a leading antibiotic 
cost $10.85 in one Washington-area 
drug store and $35.67 in another—a 
difference of more than 300 percent,” 
PhRMA said. “In other words, con- 
sumers can save 100 times the rate 
of drug inflation just by shopping 
around.” 

Critics of the pharmaceutical in-

Continued from page 13 

dustry, such as Sen. David Prior (D- 
Ariz.), contend that no amount of 
“comparison shopping” by consumers 
will protect them from the kind of 
manufacturer price increases that 
have driven the cost of many pre- 
scription products through the roof 
over the past 15 years. 

An analysis of Medi-Span data 
found that average wholesale prices 
for many top Rx products on the 
market since the 1970s increased by 
more than three times the rate of in- 
flation between 1978 and 1994. 
Those posting the biggest price in- 
creases include Dupont-Merck’s 
Coumadin, up 878 percent; Ortho’s 
Ortho-Novum, up 549 percent; 
Wyeth-Ayerst’s Premarin and Inder- 
al, both up more than 600 percent; 
Ciba-Geigy’s Lopressor, up 436 per- 
cent; and Burroughs Wellcome’s 
Lanoxin, up 1,039 percent. 


Rx inflation is cooling 


The good news, as pharmaceutical 
industry representatives are quick to 
point out, is that most of those price 
increases occurred during the free- 
wheeling 1980s. Over the past few 
years, prescription drug inflation has 
cooled down considerably. 

As recently as 1989, the U.S. De- 
partment of Labor’s Producer Price 
Index (PPI) for prescription drugs in- 


creased by an alarming 9.5 percent— 
well above the nation’s inflation rate 
as a whole, and significantly higher 
than the rise in the cost of U.S. 
health care services and products. 
Since then, however, prescription 
drug inflation has been declining 
steadily and dramatically. By 1992, 
the rise in the PPI for Rx drugs had 
dropped to 6.4, and the following 
year it was cut in half to 3.2 percent. 
Last year prescription drug producer 
price inflation fell again—to 3.0 per- 
cent, the lowest level since the early 
1970s, and preliminary indications 
are that the average may fall even 
lower for 1995. 

“For the 12 months ending in 
February, prescription drug infla- 
tion increases were 1.9 percent com- 
pared to a general inflation rate of 
2.9 percent,” PhRMA said in a series 
of consumer ads trumpeting the lat- 
est government figures. “It’s the 
lowest annual rate of pharmaceuti- 
cal inflation since December 1973,” 
and it “continues a five-year trend 
toward greater market-driven price 
moderation.” 

The pharmaceutical industry re- 
ceived more good news this spring 
when the U.S. General Accounting 
Office (GAO) concluded that, if any- 
thing, the government’s statistical 
yardstick for measuring the rise in 
pharmaceutical prices may exagger- 


ate inflation for their products. A | 
new GAO study concluded that a | 
sampling flaw in the way the govern- | 
ment checked prices of recently in- | 
troduced drugs caused the PPI “to | 
overstate drug inflation between | 
1984 and 1991 by an estimated 23 to | 
36 percent.” Moreover, the GAO con- | 
cluded that the PPI failed to reflect 

cost savings generated by consumers | 
who switched to cheaper generic | 
drugs—an error that tended to fur- | 
ther exaggerate the rise in pharma- 
ceutical manufacturer prices. “Seri- 
ous inflation in prescription drugs is 
a thing of the past,” PhRMA declared | 
in response to the latest government | 
price index figures. 


How low can they go? 


Behind the recent decline in Rx | 
price inflation: a reaction by pharma- | 
ceutical industry executives con- | 
cerned that Washington was finally | 
losing patience concerning manufac- | 
turer pricing practices. In response 
to charges from both President Clin- | 
ton and the First Lady that pharma- | 
ceutical industry “greed” was fueling | 
the rise in health care costs, 19 of the | 
nation’s leading drug manufacturers 
made an unprecedented pledge: The 
price of their prescription products 
would not rise any faster than the 
overall U.S. increase in the cost of

DRUG STORE NEWS FOR THE PHARMACIST, SEPTEMBER 1995 


COVER STORY 


Point and counterpoint 


The battie for equal access 
to pharmaceutical prices 


For retail pharmacists and 
brand name drug manufacturers, 
the chief undercurrent in the de- 
bate over prescription drug pric- 
ing has been a continuing and bit- 
ter disagreement over the “class of 
trade” discounts offered by many 
manufacturers. 

Community pharmacies, chain 
and independent alike, typically 
pay the highest prices for drugs 
because of this, and they consider 
the practice an anticompetitive 
form of price discrimination. 

Drug manufacturers, and nurs- 
ing homes, HMOs, mail service 
pharmacies and others in “man- 
aged care” who benefit from the 
discounts, contend such price re- 
ductions are appropriate rewards 
for their use of formularies to 
build market share for suppliers. 

It's a battle that’s being waged 
not only in the marketplace, but 
also in Congress, the courts and 
state legislatures across the coun- 
try. Here’s how the two sides of 
the issue shape up today: 


Pharmaceutical 
manufacturers 


As most drug manufacturers 
see it, retail pharmacy’s plan for 
legislation guaranteeing them 
“equal access” to pharmaceutical 
discounts would effectively raise 
the cost of prescription drugs 
across the board by wiping out 
discounts to everyone and estab- 
lishing a single, “unitary” price. 
Calling such “anti-discount” legis- 
lation a form of price control, offi- 
cials at the Pharmaceutical Re- 
search and Manufacturers of 
America (PhRMA) maintain that 
this approach “is inconsistent 
with the goai of strengthening 
competition in the U.S. health 
care system.” 

Although PhRMA admits that 
the price reductions that are typi- 
cally offered to HMOs and others 
are “generous,” the manufacturer 
group notes that these discounts 
are based on the ability of man- 
aged care providers to exclude 
products from their formularies. 

In contrast, retail pharmacies 


“generally stock just about all 
brands...since they must fill pre- 
scriptions written by many differ- 
ent doctors for many different pa- 
tients,” the group says. “As a 
result, their ability to bargain 
among competing manufacturers 
is more limited than that of man- 
aged care.” 


Community pharmacy 


As community pharmacy lead- 
ers see it, the discriminatory pric- 
ing practices of many pharmaceu- 
tical manufacturers force drug 
chains and independent pharma- 
cists to pay up to 10 times more 
than their managed care competi- 
tors for the same drug products. 
These enormous discounts are un- 
related to the quantities of prod- 
uct purchased and far exceed any 
reasonable compensation for the 
“formulary management” func- 
tions of institutions. 

Worse yet, officials at NACDS 
contend that drug “manufacturers 
compensate for profits lost because 
of these discounts by increasing 
the cost of products sold to commu- 
nity retail pharmacies. As a result, 
every consumer who purchases 
prescription drugs at community 
retail pharmacies must absorb the 
cost of discriminatory discounts 
given exclusively to HMOs, mail 
order pharmacies and nursing 
home pharmacy providers.” 

Far from eliminating discounts 
on pharmaceuticals by establish- 
ing a “unitary” pricing system, re- 
tail pharmacy’s plan for equal ac- 
cess would encourage the 
extension of price reductions to all 
providers. Drug stores would not 
be guaranteed the same prices as 
managed care, but they would be 
assured access to the same dis- 
counts provided they meet the 
same terms and conditions, chain 
drug industry officials say. The re- 
sult of such a shift in pharmacen- 
tical industry pricing practices 
would be to reduce the cest of pre- 
scription drugs by as much as 12 
percent, a recent analysis by the 
University of Minnesota’s PRIME 
Institute concluded. 


Reeling from 
sticker shock 


Continued from page 15 

living. The cooling of Rx price infla- 
tion during the past two years re- 
flects the willingness of most drug 
manufacturers to keep that promise. | 
“All consumers are benefiting from | 
the slowdown [in price hikes]—not 
only hospitals, health maintenance | 
organizations (HMOs) and mail or- | 
der plans, but individual consumers | 
at retail pharmacies as well,” 
PhRMA said. 

But while there’s no question that | 
prescription prices have been rising | 
more slowly during the past three | 
years, there’s considerable disagree- | 
ment about whether Rx inflation is | 
truly under control. In contrast to the 
statistics cited by drug manufactur- 
ers, NACDS officials said that “drug 
prices rose 3.3 percent in 1994, about 
22 percent more than the rate of infla- 
tion.” Additionally, chain drug indus- 
try officials are also citing the quar- 
terly results of the NACDS PRIME 
Index as further evidence that Rx in- 
flation remains a serious problem. 

The associations PRIME Index, 
compiled for NACDS by University of | 
Minnesota PRIME Institute director 
Stephen Schondelmeyer, measures 
price changes for the nation’s top 50 
prescription drugs. For the last quar- 
ter of 1994 the index found that the 
price of pharmaceuticals sold to re- 
tail pharmacies rose by 3.9 percent 
well above the overall inflation 
rate—and that the number of prod- 
ucts experiencing price increases also 
rose during that three-month period 


Research and development 


To the extent that pharmaceutical 
prices are increasing faster than the 
overall inflation rate, defenders of 
the industry say this reflects the sky- 
rocketing cost of developing new 
medicines. Under the current FDA 
g\-andrules for approving new 
drvys, it takes more than a decade of 
clinical and “pre-clinical” testing—an 
average cost of $350 million—to 
bring each new prescription product 
to the market. Streamlining the 
FDA’s leadfooted new drug approval | 
process, they say, would significantly | 
lower the cost of developing new | 
drugs and reduce the need for price 
increases on existing products to fi- | 
nance research and development 


(R&D) costs.

Wisconsin trade regulators 
urged pharmaceutical manufac- 
turers and drug wholesalers to re- 
examine their pricing practices for 
compliance with the state’s law 
banning price discrimination 
against community pharmacies 
Warning letters were issued on 
the heels of a six-month investiga- 
tion by Wisconsin’s Department of 
Agriculture, Trade and Consumer 
Protection which found that “some 
prescription drug manufacturers 
and wholesalers may be engaged 
in price discrimination against 
community pharmacies, charging 
significantly lower wholesale 
prices to hospitals, other institu 
tional pharmacies and HMOs for 
some drug products.” 

Wisconsin is one of three states 
with laws prohibiting sellers from 
offering special discounts to insti 
tutional or managed care drug 
purchasers that are not available 
on the same terms and conditions 
to all pharmacies. 

The state’s investigation ana- 
lyzed 28,869 wholesale transac- 
tions for 76 widely prescribed 
drugs and concluded that for 16 of 
these products, retail pharmacies 
were charged at least 10 percent 


SEPTEMBER 1995 


To counteract these enormous 
pricing differentials, Dorgan pro- 
posed requiring manufacturers to 


justify any difference of more than 


25 percent between the domestic 
and foreign price of a drug. If the 
manufacturer is not able to defend 
the U.S. price, Rx insurers would be 
directed to pay only the average 
cost of the product worldwide 
While manufacturers cringe at Dor 
gan’s plan, they turn absolutely 
purple when faced with proposals 
for even more direct forms of dru 
price controls

and they would work now,”

attacking such proposals. “Studies 
show that price regulation of phar

But state regulators stopped 
short of fingering any individual 
manufacturers or wholesalers for 
illegal price discrimination because 
the study incituded data from some 
hospitals that may be exempt from 
the law because they purchase 
drugs for their “own use.” 

Drug industry representatives 
seized on the inconclusive nature 
of the findings as “vindication” for 
their pricing practices. “It was an- 
other anti-consumer attempt by re- 
tail pharmacies seeking protection 
from today’s highly competitive 
marketplace,” said Pharmaceutical 
Research and Manufacturers of 
America executive vice president 
Stephen Conafay. 

Community pharmacy repre- 
sentatives read the report differ- 
ently. The results of the investiga- 
tion “provide further evidence of 
illegal manufacturer pricing 
schemes which distort competition 
in the retail pharmacy market 
and increase drug prices for con- 
sumers across the country,” 
NACDS president Ronaid L 
Ziegler said. 


by Nancy Maline 


Home health care is getting a lot 
of attention these days, but it can’t 
be too much. Statistics on the aging 
population speak for themselves. The 
1992 US Census revealed that 12.7 
percent of the population is over 65. 


States with the highest number of 


seniors include California, Florida 
and New York. Fifteen years from 
now, when the leading edge of the 
baby boom generation reaches 65, we 
will see a dramatic increase in those 
numbers 

The fastest growing age group in 
America is the 85+ group, with the 
second fastest being the 100+ group. 
It seems that people are living longer 
at a time when the country is desper- 
ately searching for ways to curb 
health care costs, which currently 
weigh in at some $750 billion annu 
ally. The solution to curbing costs 
just may be home health care

length of the average hospital stay

spending. A third of the 
sample would be satisfied 
if current spending levels

Legislation would Rep. Frank Pallone Jr. (D-NJ) is drafting new legislation to 
compensate pharmacists for cognitive services provided to 

compensate for Medicare patients. The bill, which is expected to draw strong 

a = support from pharmacy groups, will offer reimbursement for 

Medicare services three types of pharmacist services: patient counseling; 
consultations with prescribers and vaccine administration 
(in the 11 states where this is authorized). Pallone’s bill will 
be introduced this fall—perhaps as part of OBRA 95 


NACDS Wi Hill Congressional delegations from New York and Texas joined 
raiiies a chain drug industry representatives in urging GOP House 
§ leaders to continue to require pharmaceutical manufacturers to 
support or pay hefty rebates under any Medicaid “block grant” program 
approved by Congress. The Congressional Budget Office esti 
Rx rebates mates that eliminating the Medicaid rebates would cost states 
$12 billion over the next seven years—a cost that NACDS fears 
will be offset by heavy pharmacy reimbursement cuts

caid managed care programs threaten the economic 
viability” of retail pharmacies which “serve as primary 
health care providers in many inner city and rural areas

holesalers move 
eyond distribution 


By Ken Rankin 


s retail pharmacy 

fights to survive in a 

market tilting toward 
managed health care, help 
may be just a key stroke 
away. That’s the message be- 
ing beamed out to drug chains 
and independent pharmacists 
by the nation’s wholesale 
druggists. Wholesalers large 
and small alike are offering 
their customers a catalog of 
“value-added services” de- 
signed to help retail pharma- 
cy cope with today’s hyper- 
competitive marketplace. 

Indeed, over the past five 
years the wholesale drug in- 
dustry has re-invented itself 
by downplaying its traditional 
product distribution functions 
and developing new programs 
to strengthen its retail phar- 
macy customer base. They in- 
clude literally dozens of spe- 
cialized services for 
community pharmacies rang- 
ing from departmental 
“planogramming” to electronic 
inventory management to spe- 
cial contract administration 
programs for buying groups. 

Although these wholesaler-spon- 
sored programs can be a life saver 
for retail pharmacies struggling to 
compete in today’s marketplace, it’s 
not pure altruism that drives whole- 
salers to offer these services. With- 
out a healthy community pharmacy 
customer base, many wholesalers 
themselves may have trouble surviv- 
ing the managed care revolution. In 
a very real sense, the value-added 
services being offered by the indus- 
try today represent an investment by 
wholesalers in their own future. 

But not all retail pharmacies are 
taking advantage of the services being 
offered by their wholesalers. Accord- 
ing to industry leaders, part of the 
problem is that too many community 


pharmacists continue to regard whole- 
salers as little more than a source of 
product—rather than a partner in the 
provision of health care services 


The wholesale “partnership” 


Officials at the National Wholesale 
Druggists Association (NWDA) are en- 
couraging pharmacists to view whole- 
salers as more than mere “middlemen 
in the product-supply chain.” Just as 
the role of retail pharmacy is undergo- 
ing a dramatic transformation, they 
say, drug wholesaling is also re-gear- 
ing its priorities and functions in re- 
sponse to changing market realities 

“Pharmacists should think of their 
wholesalers as business partners, 
helping hands to juggle new respon- 
sibilities and consumers’ heightened

and chief executive officer Ron 
Streck. “Wholesalers can help com- 
munity pharmacists meet today’s de- 
mands while preserving them to 
meet customer needs in the future.” 
Arguably, the most valuable of the 
programs offered by wholesale drug- 
gists today are the computerized or- 
dering systems that automate the 
stock replenishment process by elec- 
tronically linking the pharmacy and 
the wholesale distribution cente1 
Sophisticated systems, such as 
McKesson’s pioneering Econolink, 
are already in their second or third 
generation, while new programs with 
even more bells, whistles and capa- 
bilities continue to emerge.

AmeriSource’s “ECHO” on-line in-

example, permits pharmacists to in- 
stantly access product and pricing 
information that is updated daily for 
thousands of SKUs.

Bergen’s “AccuSource” interactive 
computer system supplies retail cus- 
tomers with product and pricing in- 
formation on CD-ROMs. Using Accu- 
Source, pharmacists can tap into a 
vast database of information that 
even includes manufacturer-supplied 
video clips and other promotional in- 
formation on selected products. 

But ordering and inventory control 
are only two of the types of electronic 
value-added services available now 
from drug wholesalers. FoxMeyer’s 
“QuestRx” system offers on-line phar- 
macy adjudication, while Cardinal’s 
“Health Touch” in-store computer 
kiosks allow pharmacy customers to 
access drug information databases 
while waiting for their prescriptions. 

Not all wholesaler services de- 
pend on exotic, state-of-the-art, in- 
store technology, however. “Even 
the relatively low-tech telephone 
has been enhanced to be an invalu- 
able management tool,” officials at 
NWDA note. “Most wholesalers op- 
erate an 800 number where phar- 
macists can check on the availabili- 
ty of items and order them by 
pressing numbers on the touch-tone 
keypad” for delivery the next day. 


Voluntary chains 


Voluntary chain programs, such as 
Bergen Brunswig’s “Good Neighbor 
Pharmacy,” McKesson’s “Valu-Rite,” 
| AmeriSource’s “Family Pharmacy” 
| and Cardinal’s “Leader Drug Stores,” 
offer independent pharmacies many 
of the marketplace advantages en- 
joyed by large drug chains. 

Today, however, voluntaries aren’t 
the exclusive preserve of the “Big 
Six” wholesalers. Programs such as 
Kinray’s “Preferred Plus,” Value 
Drug’s “Value Health Centers” and 
The F. Dohmen Company’s “Aim 
Plus” enable customers of smaller, 
forward-looking wholesalers to reap 
the benefits of central advertising 
and merchandising. 

Wholesalers are also helping retail 
pharmacy match wits and efficien- 
cies with managed care providers by 
forming “preferred provider” net- 
works designed to keep third-party

Other wholesaler sponsored pro- 
grams can help retail pharmacy 
shave pharmaceutical acquisition 
Bergen’s “AccuSource” multi- 
media software program, for exam- 
ple, puts community pharmacies 
within a keystroke of timely and po- 
tentially valuable comparative pric- 
ing information. Cardinal’s “hx Ad- 
vantage” program offers retailers 
opportunities to increase gross mar- 
gins through purchasing contracts 
negotiated with brand name pharma- 
ceutical manufacturers. 

Smaller wholesalers are also offer- 
ing assistance to retailers via special- 
ly targeted pricing programs 
Louisiana Wholesale Drug, for one, of. 
fers its customers a retail pricing ser- 
vice for over-the-counter drugs. 

Still other wholesaler sponsored ser- 
vices are designed to help pharmacies 
build sales and profits. FoxMeyer’s 


costs. 


“Pharmasure” program enables phar- 
macists to reassure patients about the 
safety and quality of lower-cost generic 
drugs. Customers of Miami-Luken can 
bolster sales of home health care 
(HHC) lines through equipment rental 
and HHC charge card programs. At 
North Carolina Mutual, retail pharma- 
cies can lock into the company’s ag- 
gressive advertising circular program 
to help build front-end volume. 

Another service offered by a num- 
ber of wholesalers is istance in em- 
ployee training. Americource has car- 
ried this concept a step further via its 
Pharmacy Care Management Group 
which offers training programs in cog- 
nitive services for pharmacists. 

A number of companies, including 
Kinray, Smith/Texas, and Drug 
Guild, offer private label programs, 
while planogramming services are 
available from wholesalers such as 
Value Drug, North Carolina Mutual

and Bellamy Drug/King Drug. 

Even some of the smallest whole- 
salers such as McQueary Bros., a 
Missouri-vased wholesale druggist 
with just 400 retail accounts, offer 


1986 1987 1988 


store layout and design assistance to 
their pharmacy customers. 

Others are providing even more di- 
rect forms of survival assistance to 
their retail customer base. The 
Rochester Drug Co-op, for example, has 
created a holding company to purchase 


retail pharmacies with the goal of 


keeping them in operation as indepen- 
dents, rather than as part of chains. 


Wholesalers rebounding 


The provision of these value-added 
services is a key element in the cam- 
paign by drug wholesalers to build 
the kind of customer loyalty that will 
help them shore up their own sag- 
ging profit margins. And there are 
some indications that the strategy 
may be working. 

After a tough decade marked by 
increasing competition, shifting mar- 
kets and steadily declining profit 
margins, the U.S. wholesale drug in- 
dustry received some good news last 
year. For openers, total industry 
wide sales rose by a respectable 8.8 
percent to a record $51.7 billion, ac- 
cording to the latest operating sur- 
vey conducted by the NWDA. 

Industry leader McKesson Drug 
became the first wholesaler to break 
the $10 billion sales mark last year, 
while No. 2 Bergen Brunswig kept 
pace close behind with a strong sales 
surge of its own. Other major indus- 
try firms—including Cardinal 
Health, AmeriSource and Bindley 
Western—also posted impressive 
gains, as did a number of smaller 


1989 


wholesalers who have carved them- 
selves a growing niche in the drug 
distribution system. 

Although the industry’s continuing 
consolidation reduced the number of 
wholesale distribution centers in op- 


1990 


1981 1992 1993 


eration last year, net sales per ware- 
house rose by 25 percent, from an av- 
erage of $139 million to $174 million. 
The wholesale drug industry’s aver- 
age return on net worth also re- 
bounded smartly last year, rising 
from 10.15 percent of sales in 1993 to 
12.73 last year, while wholesaler op- 
erating costs fell to a new low—3.69 
percent of sales (down from 4.05 per- 
cent the year before). 


The retail connection 


Although wholesalers continued to 
expand into non-retail markets such as 
HMOs, hospitals and other institution- 
al pharmacies, many placed extra em- 
phasis on developing value-added ser- 
vices to retain their traditional 
independent and chain drug store cus- 
tomer base. As result, despite declining 
store counts among the nation’s inde- 
pendent pharmacies, wholesaler sales 
to retail customers increased last year 
by more than a billion dollars. 

Pharmaceutical manufacturer ac- 
ceptance of drug wholesalers as the 
preferred channel of distribution also 
continued to grow last year. Less 
than half of all prescription drugs 
were sold throuch wholesalers in the 
1970s, but the latest estimates indi- 
cate that the figure is now more than 
78 percent and rising. 

Best of all, perhaps, the results of 
the latest NWDA annual operating 
survey confirm that the industry was 
finally able to put a halt to declining 
profitability. If anything, NWDA’s fig- 
ures show a slight but measurable up- 


1994 


swing in profit margins for the na- 
tion’s drug distributors. After hitting 
rock bottom in 1993 at 1.64 percent of 
sales, the industry’s average pre-tax 
profit bounced back to 1,69 last year. 
Wholesaler net profits after taxes also 
rebounded from an all-time 
low of 1.03 percent of sales to 
1.15 percent last year. Accord- 
ing to some observers, this re- 
versal is the payoff from im- 
proved asset management 
techniques and an almost un- 
canny ability by wholesale 
druggists to build additional 
efficiencies in to the system. 

But while some in thé in- 

dustry regard the uptick in 
profitability as the light at the 
end of the tunnel, others see it 
as the floor at the bottom of 
the well. 

According to the pes- 
simistic view, last year’s improve- 
ment in wholesaler bottom line per- 
formance largely reflects conditions 
beyond the industry’s control—a sig- 
nificant decline in interest expenses 
that, while welcome, cannot be 
counted on to produce similar prof- 
itability increases in future years. 
Moreover, although the industry’s 
bottom line net profit performance 
showed improvement last year, aver- 
age wholesale drug gross margins 
continued to decline. Indeed, 
NWDA’s operating surveys reveal 
that in 1994 gross profit as a percent 
of sales slipped to 5.38 percent— 
nearly half a point below the year be- 
fore, and more than three points low- 
er than a decade ago. 

At leest in part, the wholesale 
drug industry’s sagging gross mar- 
gins are a direct reflection of what 
NWDA calls “the increasingly high 
level of sales to low-margin cus- 
tomers such as hospitals. clinics and 
nursing homes.” Last year, for exam- 
ple, sales by wholesale druggists to 
hospitals rose 16 percent to $13 bil- 
lion—more than 30 percent of the in- 
dustry’s total revenues for 1994. 
Sales to clinics and nursing homes 
climbed even faster—a 38 percent 
gain to nearly $1.7 billion. 

But while institutional pharmacies 
are becoming an increasingly impor- 
tant customer for many drug distrib- 
utors, retail drug stores continue to 
be the wholesale industry’s bread 
and butter. Last year purchases from

wholesalers by drug chains and inde-

95-96 forecast 


Most of the industry’s larger firms 
are bullish on prospects for 
growth-—at least over the near term. 
Cardinal Health, which ranked as 
the nation’s third largest wholesale 
druggist with sales of $6.9 billion 
during calendar year 1994, is one of 
the major industry firms that ap- 
pears to be on a roll. The Dublin, 
Ohio-based company engineered an 
impressive 23 percent sales gain for 
its latest fiscal year ending June 30, 
1995, and is banking on another 
double-digit increase on the heels of 
its recent plans to acquire Medicine 
Shoppes. 

Officials at Cardinal told Drug 
Store News For The Pharmacist that 
total sales are expected to reach “ap- 
proximately $9 billion” for the cur- 
rent fiscal year, an increase of more 
than 15 percent of the $7.8 billion 
posted during FY ’95. 

FoxMeyer, the only one of the “Big 
Six” wholesale druggists to experience 
a sales decline in calendar year 1994, 
appears to be back on track primed for 
solid growth. During the fiscal year 
that ended last March, FoxMeyer 
sales reached $5.2 billion, and compa- 
ny officials are projecting a 15 percent 
increase to $6 billion for FY ’96. 

AmeriSource is projecting double- 
digit growth for the 
coming year—a perfor- 
mance that should so- 
lidify the company’s po- 
sition as the nation’s 
fifth largest wholesale 
druggist. Althougn the 
final figures weren’t in 
at presstime, officials at 
Valley Forge, Pa.-based 
AmeriSource were esti- 
mating that total sales 
for the year ending 
Sept. 30, 1995 would 
reach $4.8 billion—up 
some 12 percent from 
FY 1994’s $4.3 billion.

on these services?

drug companies have devel 
oped an array of value 
added services to help retail 
pharmacists cut costs, im 
prove sales and remain com 
petitive in today’s prescrip 
tion drug marketplace 
Although not all wholesalers 
provide the same programs 
the National Wholesale 
Druggists Association lists 
the follow services as among 
the most popular offered 

® Reimbursement for 
cognitive services pro 
vides essential training 
computer programs and 
marketing assistance di 
rected toward patients 
payors and practitioners 
that enables pharma ists 
to be paid for cognitive and 
value-added services

bursement services 
training, enhanced comput 
er software programs and 
other assistance to help se 
cure reimbursement process 
for pharmaceutical care

training and information 
provides literature and con 
tinuing education to sharp 
en counseling skills 

® Management infor- 
mation reports analyz 
sales and profits, identify 
and follow up on selling op 
portunities and monitor via 
computer inventory levels 
and ordering patterns 

® Automated inven- 
tory control includes bar 
coded shelf labels and the 
automatic generation of 
price stickers to increase 
inventory turnover and re 
duce inventory investment 

® Electronic order en- 
try will transmit orders 
directly to a wholesale: 
computer. Eliminates paper 
work, reduces order prepa 
ration time and speeds up 
delivery

ing and promotions: Ad 
vertising circulars, news 
paper, radio and TV 


advertising, window ban- 
ners and point-of-purchase 
materials increase store 
traffic and sales

tising coordinates the pool 
ing of co-op money from dif 
ferent pharmacies to 
develop large-scale advertis 
ing programs 

® Identity programs 
where a common logo is de
